bins.

8
Reduction and Reconstruction of the Instance

In this section we present algorithms
Reduce
and
Reconstruct
, which handle the structuring of the instance and
the transformation of the solution for the structured instance into a solution for the original one. For this section, fix
{\mathcal{J}}=(I,\mathcal{G},s,k)
to be a BPP instance, and let
{\varepsilon}\in(0,0.1]
.

Algorithm
Reduce
generates from a given instance
{\mathcal{J}}
a structured instance
{\mathcal{I}}
, for which any optimal packing required at most the minimum number of bins required for packing
{\mathcal{J}}

(see Section
8.1
). Given a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
, algorithm
Reconstruct
finds a packing of the original instance
{\mathcal{J}}
, with only a slight increase in the total number of bins used (see Section
8.2
). The proofs of the results in this section are given in Appendix
G
.

8.1
The Reduction

Towards structuring the instance, we first classify the items and groups in
{\mathcal{J}}
.
We note that similar classifications were used in prior work (see, e.g.,
[
11
,
21
,
13
]
).
For all
i\in\{2,\ldots,{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1\}
let

I_{i}=\big\{\ell\in I~|~s(\ell)\in\big[{\varepsilon}^{i+1},{\varepsilon}^{i}\big)\big\}

(17)

be the
i
-th
interval
of
{\mathcal{J}}
, containing all items of sizes in the interval
[{\varepsilon}^{i+1},{\varepsilon}^{i})
; we refer to
i
is a
pivot
of
{\mathcal{J}}
. Now, for
w\in\{2,\ldots,{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1\}
, we say that
w
is a
minimal pivot
of
\mathcal{{\mathcal{J}}}
if the
w
-th interval of
{\mathcal{J}}
contains minimal total size of items among all intervals of
{\mathcal{J}}
; that is,

w=\argmin_{i\in\{2,\ldots,{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1\}}s(I_{i}).

(18)

The classification of the items depends on the selection of a pivot for
{\mathcal{J}}
. Specifically, given
w\in\{2,\ldots,{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1\}
we classify item
\ell
as
w
-
heavy
if
s(\ell)\geq\varepsilon^{w}
,
w
-
medium
if
s(\ell)\in[\varepsilon^{w+1},\varepsilon^{w})
and
w
-
light
otherwise. Let
H_{w}=\{\ell\in I~|~s(\ell)\geq{\varepsilon}^{w}\}
be the set of
w
-heavy items in
{\mathcal{J}}
.

For the classification of groups, fix a pivot
w\in\{2,\ldots,{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1\}
of
{\mathcal{J}}
. We sort the groups in
\mathcal{G}
in a non-increasing order by the total number of
w
-heavy and
w
-medium items of the group; then, we classify the first up to
{\varepsilon}^{-3w-5}
groups according to the above order as the
w
-
large groups
, and the remaining groups are called
w
-
small
. More formally, let
g_{w}(G)=|G\cap(H_{w}\cup I_{w})|~:~\forall G\in{\mathcal{G}}
and
n=|{\mathcal{G}}|
. Now, let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{n}
be a
non-increasing order of
{\mathcal{G}}
by
g_{w}
, where
groups having the same
g_{w}
values are placed in fixed arbitrary order.
Now, let
\kappa_{w}=\min\left\{{\varepsilon}^{-3w-5},|{\mathcal{G}}|\right\}
and define the set of
w
-large groups as
{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)=\left\{G_{i}~|~i\in\left[\kappa_{w}\right]\right\}
and the set of
w
-small groups as
{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}
.

Lemma 8.1
.

For all
w\in\{2,\ldots,{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1\}
there are at most
{\varepsilon}^{-3w-5}
groups
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that

|G\cap(H_{w}\cup I_{w})|\geq{\varepsilon}^{2w+4}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}}).

The proof of Lemma
8.1
follows by noting that
the total size of items in a group having at least
{\varepsilon}^{2w+4}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})
items that are
w
-medium or
w
-heavy is at least
{\varepsilon}^{3w+5}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})
.

By Lemma
8.1
, the number of
w
-heavy and
w
-medium items in a
w
-small group is at most
{\varepsilon}^{2w+4}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})
. This is useful for the reduction, in which the matroid constraint is slightly relaxed for
w
-small groups, as described below. The
w
-large groups appear in the reduced instance with the original cardinality constraint. In contrast, for each
w
-small group we keep only the
w
-light and
w
-medium items with the original cardinality constraint of the group, whereas all
w
-heavy items from
w
-small groups are placed in a single
union group
with unbounded cardinality constraint. Specifically, define the
reduced
w
-small groups
containing only
w
-light and
w
-medium items as

{\mathcal{G}}_{S}(w)=\{G\setminus H_{w}~|~G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)\}.

(19)

Also, let the
w
-union group
containing all
w
-heavy items from
w
-small groups be

\Gamma_{w}=\{\ell\in H_{w}~|~\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)\}.

(20)

1

Find a minimal pivot
w\in\{2,\ldots,{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1\}
of
{\mathcal{J}}
.

2

Find
{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w),{\mathcal{G}}_{S}(w),\Gamma_{w}
, the
w
-large groups,
w
-reduced small groups, and
w
-union of
{\mathcal{J}}
.

3

Let
{\mathcal{G}}_{R}={\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)\cup{\mathcal{G}}_{S}(w)\cup\{\Gamma_{w}\}
.

4

Define the following new cardinality bounds for all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{R}
:

k_{R}(G)=\begin{cases}k(G)&G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)\\
k(G^{\prime})&G=G^{\prime}\setminus H_{w}\text{ s.t. }G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)\\
|\Gamma_{w}|+1&G=\Gamma_{w}.\\
\end{cases}

5

Return the reduced instance
\left(I,{\mathcal{G}}_{R},s,k_{R}\right)
.

6

Algorithm 6

\textsf{Reduce}({\varepsilon},{\mathcal{J}}=(I,{\mathcal{G}},s,k))

Algorithm
Reduce
constructs the above groups, together with the new cardinality constraints, and preserves the initial set of items and item sizes. The pseudocode of Algorithm
Reduce
is given in Algorithm
6
. The first claim of Lemma
2.2
holds since the only of groups containing items larger than
{\varepsilon}^{2}
are the
w
-large groups and the
w
-union group. Moreover, a packing of the original instance is also a feasible packing for the reduced instance, thus the optimum of the reduced instance can only be smaller.

8.2
The Reconstruction

We now describe how a packing of the reduced instance is transformed to a packing for the original instance.
For simplicity, assume that objects such as
w
computed by algorithm
\textsf{Reduce}({\varepsilon},{\mathcal{J}})
are known and fixed for the reconstruction of
{\mathcal{J}}
. In addition, for the remainder of this section, fix a packing
A=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m})
for
{\mathcal{I}}=\textsf{Reduce}({\varepsilon},{\mathcal{J}})
.

Recall that Step
5
of algorithm
Reduce
relaxes the cardinality constraint for the
w
-heavy items in the set
\Gamma_{w}
. The reconstruction algorithm redefines
A
for items in
\Gamma_{w}
to ensure that the matroid constraint is satisfied for these items; this may require using a few extra bins. Then,
w
-medium and
w
-light items from
w
-small groups are discarded from the packing and packed separately using Algorithm
Greedy
(see Lemma
1.2
). This results in a feasible packing of the original instance
{\mathcal{J}}
.
We now describe how our reconstruction algorithm resolves violations among
w
-heavy items. This is done by rearranging the packing of
w
-heavy items in
A
using a variant of the classic
linear shifting
technique of
[
39
]
.
Let
\beta={\varepsilon}^{-w-2}
be the
shifting parameter
, and
P_{1},\ldots,P_{q}
the
\mathcal{{\mathcal{I}}}
-
partition
obtained by applying linear shifting to the items in
\Gamma_{w}
. More specifically, given the set
\Gamma_{w}
in non-decreasing order of item sizes, for all
1\leq i\leq j<q
and
\ell\in P_{i},y\in P_{j}
it holds that
s(\ell)\geq s(y)
,
|P_{i}|={\left\lceil\frac{|\Gamma_{w}|}{\beta}\right\rceil}
, and
P_{q}
contains the remaining (possibly less than
{\left\lceil\frac{|\Gamma_{w}|}{\beta}\right\rceil}
) items from
\Gamma_{w}
. It follows that
q\leq\beta
and let
q({\mathcal{I}})=q
be the number of sets in the above partition of
\Gamma_{w}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

There is an algorithm
Fill
which given a BPP instance
{\mathcal{J}}
and a packing
A=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m})
of
{\mathcal{I}}=\textnormal{{Reduce}}({\varepsilon},{\mathcal{J}})
finds in time
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{J}}|,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
a partition
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m},R)
of
\Gamma_{w}
such that the following hold.

1.

For all
i\in[m]
and
j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})\}
it holds that
|B_{i}\cap P_{j}|\leq|A_{i}\cap P_{j-1}|
and
|B_{i}\cap P_{1}|=0
.

2.

For all
i\in[m]
and
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that

|B_{i}\cap G|\leq k(G)
.

3.

|R|\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})+1
.

The first condition of the lemma is essentially a shifting argument. The lemma is proved by constructing the following greedy algorithm
Fill
. We start with empty sets for
B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}
and with
R=\Gamma_{w}
. In each iteration, we try to move an item from
R
to some
B_{i},i\in[m]
without violating the conditions of Lemma
8.2
.
If no move is possible, we return
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m},R)
. The details are given in Appendix
G.3
.

We now combine the output of algorithm
Fill
with
A
. We first remove from
A
the items in
\Gamma_{w}
and all
w
-medium items from
w
-small groups; then, for all
i\in[m]
we add to
A_{i}
the items in
B_{i}
. The remaining items in
R
are packed using extra bins. Formally, let

\Omega_{w}=\{\ell\in I_{w}~|~\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)\}

(21)

be the
w
-medium items from
w
-small groups. In addition, given two tuples
T_{1},T_{2}
, let
T_{1}\oplus T_{2}
be the concatenation of
T_{1}
by
T_{2}
. Now, given
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m},R)=\textsf{Fill}(A)
, define the tuple

F_{A}=\big((A_{i}\setminus(\Gamma_{w}\cup\Omega_{w}))\cup B_{i}~|~i\in[m]\big)\oplus\big(\{\ell\}~|~\ell\in R\big).

(22)

In the following, we transform
F_{A}
into a feasible packing of
{\mathcal{J}}
. Let
F_{A}=(U_{1},\ldots,U_{m^{\prime}})
. Observe that Lemma
8.2
does not guarantee that
F_{A}

satisfies the cardinality constraints for all the
w
-small groups.
Such violations are resolved by discarding
w
-light items from
U_{i},i\in[m^{\prime}]
to ensure that the cardinality constraints of all groups in
{\mathcal{G}}
are satisfied. More specifically, for any bin
i\in[m^{\prime}]
and group
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
let
F_{A}(i,G)
be an arbitrary exclusion-minimal subset of
w
-light items from
U_{i}
such that
|(U_{i}\setminus F_{A}(i,G))\cap G|\leq k(G)
. Note that such a subset exists, since by taking all
w
-light items to
F_{A}(i,G)
, the above condition is satisfied by Lemma
8.2
and (
22
). Now, the discarded items are accumulated across all bins and groups to form the set
D(A)
, namely

D(A)=\bigcup_{i\in[m^{\prime}]}\bigcup_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}F_{A}(i,G).

(23)

Lemma 8.3
.

For any packing
A
of
{\mathcal{I}}
,

1.

|D(A)\cap G|\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})
for all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
.

2.

s(D(A))\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})
.

The first condition in the lemma follows from Lemma
8.1
, since we do not discard items from
w
-large groups. The second condition holds since any
w
-heavy item is larger than any
w
-light item by factor at least
{\varepsilon}^{-1}
. These properties are useful for packing the discarded items in only a few extra bins using algorithm
Greedy
.

Given the partial packing of
{\mathcal{J}}
, algorithm
Reconstruct
proceeds to pack the remaining items, i.e., the
w
-medium items from
w
-small groups and items in
D(A)
. All of these items are packed in a few extra bins.
This is done by algorithm
Greedy
, for which we define a residual instance containing only the above remaining items, which preserves the item sizes and group cardinality constraints for the remaining items. Formally, define the
discarded instance
of
{\mathcal{J}}
and
A
as
\mathcal{E}=(E,{\mathcal{G}}_{E},s_{E},k_{E})
, where

\displaystyle E=\Omega_{w}\cup D(A),~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\mathcal{G}_{E}=\{G\cap E~|~G\in{\mathcal{G}}\},

(24)

\displaystyle s_{E}(\ell)=s(\ell),~~\forall\ell\in E,~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~k_{E}(G\cap E)=k(G),~~\forall G\in{\mathcal{G}}

Given
S\subseteq I
and a tuple
T=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{n})
such that
T_{i}\subseteq I
for all
i\in[n]
, let
T\setminus S=(T_{1}\setminus S,\ldots,T_{n}\setminus S)
be the tuple induced by removing the items in
S
from all entries of
T
. The pseudocode of algorithm
Reconstruct
is given in Algorithm
7
. By (
22
) and (
24
), the output of algorithm
Reconstruct
is a packing of
{\mathcal{J}}
. Furthermore, this packing is of size at most
m+13{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})+1
by Lemmas
8.2
,
8.3
, and
8.1
.

1

Generate
F_{A}
using
\textsf{Fill}(\mathcal{J},A)
and (
22
).

2

Compute the discarded instance
\mathcal{E}=(E,{\mathcal{G}}_{E},s_{E},k_{E})
of
{\mathcal{J}}
and
A
.

3

Return
\left(F_{A}\setminus E\right)\oplus\textsf{Greedy}(\mathcal{E})
.

4

Algorithm 7

\textsf{Reconstruct}(\mathcal{J},{\varepsilon},A)

9
Discussion

In this paper we presented
an
o(\textnormal{OPT})
additive approximation algorithm for Bin Packing with Partition Matroid. While BPP is a natural generalization of Bin Packing variants that have been
studied in the past, to the best of our knowledge it is studied here for the first time.
Our result improves upon the APTAS of
[
13
]
for the special case of Group Bin Packing and generalizes the AFPTAS of
[
16
]
for the special case of Bin Packing with Cardinality Constraints. Our algorithm is based on rounding a solution for the configuration-LP formulation of the problem. The rounding process relies on the key notion of a
prototype
, in which items are placeholders for other items, and the use of fractional grouping
[
17
]
. Our algorithm demonstrates the power of this fractional version of linear grouping in solving constrained packing problems; it also
shows how fractional grouping can be used
constructively
.

While our algorithm outputs a solution which uses
\textnormal{OPT}+o(\textnormal{OPT})
bins, the function hidden by the little-o notation is of the form
\frac{x}{(\ln\ln x)^{\frac{1}{17}}}
. We believe that a tighter analysis may lead to a better additive approximation,
for example, to an algorithm which returns a solution using
\textnormal{OPT}+O\left(\frac{\textnormal{OPT}}{\ln\textnormal{OPT}}\right)
bins. We leave the tighter analysis for the full version of this paper. The existence of approximation algorithms for BPP which return a solution using at most
\textnormal{OPT}+O(\textnormal{OPT}^{1-{\varepsilon}})
bins, for some constant
{\varepsilon}>0
, remains open.

The techniques presented in this paper seem to be useful also in other settings.
Our preliminary study suggests we can apply these techniques
to obtain a polynomial time approximation scheme for
Multiple Knapsack with Partition Matroid
, a generalization of the Multiple Knapsack problem (see, e.g.,
[
8
,
31
]
) in which the items assigned to each bin form an independent set of a partition matroid. Another application comes from the design of approximation algorithms for
Machine Scheduling with Partition Matroid,
a generalization of the classic Machine Scheduling problem in which the jobs assigned to a machine must be an independent set of a given partition matroid. We note that the problem is a generalization of Machine Scheduling with Bag-Constraints studied in
[
11
,
21
]
.

The problem of Bin Packing with Partition Matroid is a special case of Bin Packing with Matroid, for which the input is a set of items
I
, a size function
s:I\to\mathbb{R}
and a matroid
{\mathcal{M}}
. The objective is to partition
I
into a minimal number of bins
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
such that
A_{b}
is an independent set of the matroid
{\mathcal{M}}
, and
s(A_{b})\leq 1
for all
b\in[m]
. This problem is a natural generalization of both Bin Packing and Matroid Partitioning; yet, we were unable to find any published results.
We note that our approach for solving BPP heavily relies on the structure of the partition matroid, and therefore cannot be easily extended to handle a general matroid.

Appendix A
Omitted Proofs of Section
3

Proof of Lemma
3.1
:

The approach presented here is considered as standard, and often the result is mentioned without a proof (see, e.g., Theorem 1.1 in
[
5
]
). We include the proof
for completeness. We refer to terms such as separation oracle and well-described polyhedron as defined in
[
23
]
.

Let
{\mathcal{I}}=(I,{\mathcal{G}},s,k)
be a BPP instance and
{\varepsilon}\in(0,0.1)
.
For any
\mathcal{D}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}
we define the following linear program.

\textnormal{LP}(\mathcal{D}):~~~~~\begin{aligned} ~~~~~\min\quad&~~~~~\sum_{C\in\mathcal{D}}\bar{x}_{C}\\
\textsf{s.t.\quad}&~~~\sum_{~C\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]\cap\mathcal{D}}\bar{x}_{C}\geq 1&\forall\ell\in I~~~~~\\
&~~~~~\bar{x}_{C}\geq 0~~~~~~~~&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\forall C\in\mathcal{C}~~~~\end{aligned}

(25)

While
\textnormal{LP}({\mathcal{C}})
is not identical to (
1
), solving
\textnormal{LP}({\mathcal{C}})
is equivalent to solving (
1
) and we will focus on this objective.

For any
\mathcal{D}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}
, the dual linear program of
\textnormal{LP}(\mathcal{D})
is the following.

\textnormal{Dual}(\mathcal{D}):~~~~~\begin{aligned} ~~~~~\max\quad&~~~~~\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}\\
\textsf{s.t.\quad}&~~~\sum_{\ell\in C}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}\leq 1&\forall C\in\mathcal{D}~~~~~\\
&~~~~~\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}\geq 0~~~~~~~~&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\forall\ell\in I~~~~\end{aligned}

(26)

We note that
\textnormal{Dual}({\mathcal{C}})
can be solved in polynomial time given a separation oracle (see Theorem 6.3.2 in
[
23
]
). However, since no such separation oracle exists, we apply a technique dating back to
[
34
]
in order to obtain an approximate solution.

Given
\mathcal{D}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}
, define a polytope

F(\mathcal{D},v)=\left\{\bar{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{I}_{\geq 0}~~\middle|~~\begin{aligned} \sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}\geq v\\
\sum_{\ell\in C}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}\leq 1&~~~~~~~\forall C\in\mathcal{D}\end{aligned}\right\}

(27)

Clearly,
\textnormal{OPT}(\textnormal{Dual}(\mathcal{D}))\geq v
if and only if
F(\mathcal{D},v)\neq\emptyset
.

Observe that, for every
\mathcal{D}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, each of the inequalities in the definitions
F(\mathcal{D},v)
can be represented using
\varphi=O(|I|+\|v\|)
bits, where
\|v\|
is the size of the representation for the number
v
; thus,
(F(\mathcal{D},v),n,\varphi)
is a well-described polyhedron (Definition 6.2.2 in
[
23
]
).
By Theorem 6.4.1 in
[
23
]
there is an algorithm
Ellipsoid
which given a separation oracle for
F(\mathcal{D},v)
determines if
F(\mathcal{D},v)\neq\emptyset
in time
\textnormal{poly}(|I|,\|v\|)
.

We use
Ellipsoid
to determine if
F({\mathcal{C}},v)\neq\emptyset
with the following (flawed) separation oracle. Given
\bar{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{I}_{\geq 0}
, the oracle first checks if
\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}<v
. If this is the case, the algorithm returns
\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda_{\ell}}<v
as a separating hyperplane. Otherwise, the algorithm runs the FPTAS for CMP with the instance
{\mathcal{I}}
, the weight function
w(\ell)=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}
and
\frac{{\varepsilon}}{10}
as the error. If the FPTAS returned
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\sum_{\ell\in C}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}>1
, the algorithm returns
\sum_{\ell\in C}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}>1
as a separating hyperplane. If the configuration returned by the FPTAS does not meet this condition, then the oracle aborts the execution of
Ellipsoid
.

Observe that the execution of the separation oracle runs in time
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{I}}|,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}},\|v\|)
. Thus, the execution of
Ellipsoid
with the oracle terminates in polynomial time. The execution can either end with a declaration that
F({\mathcal{C}},v)=\emptyset
or be aborted by the separation oracle. Consider each of these two cases:

•

The execution terminated by declaring that
F({\mathcal{C}},v)=\emptyset
. Then, as the separating hyperplanes returned by the oracle are indeed separating hyperplanes, it follows that
F({\mathcal{C}},v)=\emptyset
is a correct statement. Let
\mathcal{D}
be the set of configurations returned as separating hyperplanes throughout the execution. Then, as all the separating hyperplanes returned are separating hyperplanes for
F(\mathcal{D},v)
we conclude that
F(\mathcal{D},v)=\emptyset
as well. Furthermore,
|\mathcal{D}|
is polynomial in
|I|
and
\|v\|
, as the running time of
Ellipsoid
is polynomial in these variables.

•

Otherwise, the execution of
Ellipsoid
has been aborted. Let
\bar{\lambda}
be the value given to the separation oracle on its last call (the one which ended up with the abortion). It follows that
\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}\geq v
and
\sum_{\ell\in C}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}\leq\frac{1}{1-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{10}}
for all
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
(otherwise the FPTAS must return a solution
C
for which
\sum_{\ell\in C}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}>1
). Then it holds that
\left(1-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{10}\right)\bar{\lambda}\in F\left({\mathcal{C}},\left(1-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{10}\right)\cdot v\right)
, and consequently
F\left({\mathcal{C}},\left(1-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{10}\right)\cdot v\right)\neq\emptyset
.

Thus, using a binary search, we can find
v\in[0,n]
and
\mathcal{D}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}
such that
F({\mathcal{C}},v)=F(\mathcal{D},v)=\emptyset
,
F\left({\mathcal{C}},\left(1-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\cdot v\right)\neq\emptyset
, and
|\mathcal{D}|
is polynomial in
|I|
. As
F\left({\mathcal{C}},\left(1-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\cdot v\right)\neq\emptyset
it follows that
\textnormal{OPT}(\textnormal{Dual}({\mathcal{C}}))\geq\left(1-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\cdot v
, and by strong duality it holds that
\textnormal{OPT}(\textnormal{LP}({\mathcal{C}}))\geq\left(1-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\cdot v
. Furthermore, as
F({\mathcal{C}},v)=F(\mathcal{D},v)=\emptyset
it follows that
\textnormal{OPT}(\textnormal{Dual}(\mathcal{D}))\leq v
, and by strong duality
\textnormal{OPT}(\textnormal{LP}(\mathcal{D}))\leq v
. As
|\mathcal{D}|
is polynomial we can solve
\textnormal{LP}(\mathcal{D})
in polynomial time and obtain a solution
{\bar{x}}
(which is also a solution for
\textnormal{LP}({\mathcal{C}})
), such that

\|{\bar{x}}\|=\textnormal{OPT}(\textnormal{LP}(\mathcal{D}))\leq v\leq\frac{\textnormal{OPT}(\textnormal{LP}({\mathcal{C}}))}{1-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}}\leq(1+{\varepsilon})\cdot\textnormal{OPT}(\textnormal{LP}({\mathcal{C}})).

Overall, we obtained a
(1+{\varepsilon})
-approximate solution in
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{I}}|,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
time, as required.

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.11
:

Observe that the optimum of (
1
) is at most
\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{I}})
, thus by Lemma
3.1
it holds that
\bar{x}
is a solution to the configuration LP (
1
) of
\mathcal{I}
and
\|{\bar{x}}\|\leq(1+{\varepsilon})\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{I})
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.6
, Algorithm
\textsf{Evict}({\varepsilon},{\mathcal{I}},\bar{x})
in Step
1
returns a prototype
\bar{y}
with
\bar{\gamma}
in the
\bar{y}
-polytope such that (i) for all
\ell,j\in I,\ell\neq j
it holds that
\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}=0
; (ii) for all
C\in\textnormal{{supp}}(\bar{y})
it holds that
|C|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10}
and
s(C\setminus L)\leq{\varepsilon}
; (iii)
\|\bar{y}\|\leq(1+{\varepsilon})\|\bar{x}\|
; (iv) for all
\ell\in I
it holds that
\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[\ell]}{\bar{y}}_{C}\leq 2
. Then, in Step
1
, Algorithm
\textsf{Shift}({\varepsilon},{\mathcal{I}},\bar{y})
returns a good prototype
\bar{z}
by Lemma
3.7
such that

\displaystyle\|\bar{z}\|\leq{}

\displaystyle(1+5{\varepsilon})\|\bar{y}\|+Q({\varepsilon})\leq(1+5{\varepsilon})\cdot(1+{\varepsilon})\cdot\|\bar{x}\|+Q({\varepsilon})

(28)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle(1+9{\varepsilon})(1+{\varepsilon})\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{I})+Q({\varepsilon})\leq(1+19{\varepsilon})\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{I})+Q({\varepsilon}).

The first inequality is by Lemma
3.7
. The second inequality is by Lemma
3.6
. The third inequality is by Lemma
3.1
and because
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
. Consequently, by Lemma
3.9
, in Step
1
it holds that
\mathcal{B}
is an
{\varepsilon}
-nice partition with size at most:

\|\bar{z}\|+{\varepsilon}^{-22}Q^{2}({\varepsilon})\leq(1+19{\varepsilon})\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{I})+Q({\varepsilon})+{\varepsilon}^{-22}Q^{2}({\varepsilon})\leq(1+19{\varepsilon})\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{I})+{\varepsilon}^{-23}Q^{2}({\varepsilon})

(29)

The first inequality is (
28
). The second inequality is because
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
. Then, by Lemma
3.10
, in Step
1
, a full packing
\Phi
of
\mathcal{I}
is constructed. The number of bins in
\Phi
is bounded by:

\displaystyle\#\textsf{bins}(\Phi)\leq{}

\displaystyle(1+2{\varepsilon})\left((1+19{\varepsilon})\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{I})+{\varepsilon}^{-23}Q^{2}({\varepsilon})\right)+2{\varepsilon}^{-22}Q^{2}({\varepsilon})\leq(1+60{\varepsilon})\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{I})+3{\varepsilon}^{-23}Q^{2}({\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle(1+60{\varepsilon})\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{I})+{\varepsilon}^{-24}Q^{2}({\varepsilon})\leq(1+60{\varepsilon})\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{I})+Q^{3}({\varepsilon}).

The first inequality is by (
29
) and Lemma
3.10
. The other inequalities hold as
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
. ∎

\mathcal{I}

Compute
\Phi=\textsf{AFPTAS}({\mathcal{I}},{\varepsilon})
(Algorithm
1

Return a packing for
{\cal J}

A=\textsf{Reconstruct}({\mathcal{J}},{\varepsilon},\Phi)

Algorithm 8

\textnormal{{Gen-AFPTAS}}({{\mathcal{J}}},{\varepsilon})

The pseudo-code of
Gen-AFPTAS

8
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
:

Let
\mathcal{J}=(I,\mathcal{G},s,k)
be a BPP instance. Recall that
V({\mathcal{J}})=\max_{G\in\mathcal{G}}{\left\lceil\frac{|G|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}
is the maximum cardinality of a group in
\mathcal{J}
divided by the cardinality constraint of the group and let
W=s(I)+V({\mathcal{J}})+c
where
c=\exp\left(\exp\left({100^{17}}\right)\right)
is a large constant.

Claim A.1
.

\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})\leq 2\cdot W

Proof.

Let
L_{\frac{1}{2}}=\{\ell\in I~|~s(\ell)>\frac{1}{2}\}
. By Lemma
1.2
it follows that
Greedy
finds a packing of the instance
{\mathcal{J}}\setminus L_{\frac{1}{2}}
using
at most

\left(1+2\cdot\frac{1}{2}\right)\cdot\max\left\{s(I\setminus L_{\frac{1}{2}}),V({\mathcal{J}}\setminus L_{\frac{1}{2}})\right\}+2\leq 2\cdot\max\left\{s(I\setminus L_{\frac{1}{2}}),V({\mathcal{J}})\right\}+2

bins, where we define
{\mathcal{J}}\setminus L_{\frac{1}{2}}=(I_{L_{\frac{1}{2}}},{\mathcal{G}}_{L_{\frac{1}{2}}},s_{L_{\frac{1}{2}}},k_{L_{\frac{1}{2}}})
as the BPP instance such that

\displaystyle I_{L_{\frac{1}{2}}}=I\setminus L_{\frac{1}{2}},~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\mathcal{G}_{L_{\frac{1}{2}}}=\{G\cap I_{L_{\frac{1}{2}}}\neq\emptyset~|~G\in{\mathcal{G}}\},

\displaystyle s_{L_{\frac{1}{2}}}(\ell)=s(\ell),~~\forall\ell\in I_{L_{\frac{1}{2}}},~~~~~~~~~~~k_{L_{\frac{1}{2}}}(G\cap I_{L_{\frac{1}{2}}})=k(G),~~\forall G\in{\mathcal{G}}\text{ s.t. }G\cap I_{L_{\frac{1}{2}}}\neq\emptyset.

The items in
L_{\frac{1}{2}}
can be packed into
|L_{\frac{1}{2}}|\leq 2\cdot s\left(L_{\frac{1}{2}}\right)
bins (that is, partitioned into
|L_{\frac{1}{2}}|
configurations) with a single item per bin. Thus, set of items
I
can be packed into number of bins bounded by

2\cdot\max\left\{s(I\setminus L_{\frac{1}{2}}),V({\mathcal{J}})\right\}+2+2\cdot s\left(L_{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\leq 2\left(s(I)+V({\mathcal{J}})\right)+2\leq 2\cdot W

∎

Now, define
{\varepsilon}=\left\lfloor\left(\ln\ln W\right)^{\frac{1}{17}}\right\rfloor^{-1}
. Since
W\geq c
, it holds that
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
.
In the following we show that the running time of
Gen-AFPTAS
on the input
\mathcal{J}
and
{\varepsilon}
, as defined above, is polynomial in
|\mathcal{J}|
, while the number of bins in the packing returned by the algorithm is
\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})+o(\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J}))
.

By Lemma
3.12
, there are polynomial functions
f,g
such that the running time of
\textnormal{{Gen-AFPTAS}}\left(\mathcal{J},{\varepsilon}\right)
is at most
f\left(\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\right)\cdot g(|\mathcal{J}|)
. We assume without the loss of generality that
f
is monotone. Therefore,

f\left(\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\right)\cdot g(|\mathcal{J}|)=f\left(\left\lfloor\left(\ln\ln W\right)^{\frac{1}{17}}\right\rfloor\right)\cdot g(|\mathcal{J}|)\leq f(W)\cdot g(|\mathcal{J}|)\leq f(2\cdot|{\mathcal{J}}|+c)\cdot g(|{\mathcal{J}}|)

(30)

The first equality is by the definition of
{\varepsilon}
. The first inequality is because
W>c>1
and for all
x\geq 1
it holds that
\ln x\leq x
. The last inequality hold as
V({\mathcal{I}}),s(I)\leq|I|\leq|{\mathcal{J}}|
.
Since
c
is a constant it follows from the (
30
) that the running time of
\textnormal{{Gen-AFPTAS}}({\mathcal{J}},{\varepsilon})
is polynomial in
|{\mathcal{J}}|
.

By Lemma
3.12
, it holds that the packing
A
returned by
\textsf{Gen-AFPTAS}\left(\mathcal{J},{\varepsilon}\right)
is a packing of
\mathcal{J}
which uses at most
(1+130{\varepsilon})OPT(\mathcal{I})+3\cdot Q^{3}({\varepsilon})
bins.
Now,

\displaystyle Q({\varepsilon})={}

\displaystyle\exp({\varepsilon}^{-17})=\exp\left(\left(\left\lfloor\left(\ln\ln W\right)^{\frac{1}{17}}\right\rfloor^{-1}\right)^{-17}\right)

(31)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\exp\left(\ln\ln W\right)=\ln W\leq\ln\left(2\cdot\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})+c\right).

The first equality is by the definition of
Q({\varepsilon})
. The second equality is by the definition of
{\varepsilon}
. The last inequality holds by
\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})\geq s(I)
,
\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})\geq V({\mathcal{I}})
and the definition of
W
. Hence, the number of bins used by
A
is at most

\displaystyle

\displaystyle(1+130{\varepsilon})\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})+3\cdot Q^{3}({\varepsilon})

(32)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})+130\left\lfloor\left(\ln\ln W\right)^{\frac{1}{17}}\right\rfloor^{-1}\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})+3\cdot\ln^{3}\left(2\cdot\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})+c\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})+130\cdot\frac{\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})}{\left(\ln\ln\left(\frac{\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})}{2}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{17}}-1}+3\cdot\ln^{3}\left(2\cdot\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})+c\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})+O\left(\frac{\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})}{\left(\ln\ln\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})\right)^{\frac{1}{17}}}\right).

The first inequality is by the definition of
{\varepsilon}
and (
31
). The second inequality holds since
W\geq\frac{\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})}{2}
by Claim
A.1
.

Overall, we showed that the algorithm returns a packing of
{\mathcal{J}}
using
\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})+O\left(\frac{\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})}{\left(\ln\ln\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})\right)^{\frac{1}{17}}}\right)
bins in polynomial time in
|{\mathcal{J}}|
. ∎

Appendix B
Properties of the
\bar{x}
-polytope

In this section we give the proof of Lemma
3.4
. Let
\bar{x}
be a prototype of
\mathcal{I}
which satisfy the conditions of Lemma
3.4
: there is an integer
k
such that for all
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})
it holds that
|C|\leq k
and
\bar{x}_{C}\in\mathbb{N}
; also, let
\bar{\lambda}
be a vertex of the
\bar{x}
-polytope for which constraint (
8
) holds with equality. We say that a constraint is
tight
if it holds with equality. Define the
active
types of
\bar{x}
as

T=\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})\cup\{j\in I~|~\exists C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})\text{ s.t. }j\in C\}.

For every
t\in T
and
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
, we define below a value
L_{t,G}
. Let
C\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap T
,
j\in I\cap T
, and
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
. Define
L_{C,G}=\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\left(k(G)-|G\cap C|\right)
and
L_{j,G}=\sum_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}[j]}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}
. For every
(\ell,t)\in I\times(I\cup{\mathcal{C}})
, we say the entry
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}

corresponds
to
\ell
and
t
. Note that all entries
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}
such that
t\in(I\cup{\mathcal{C}})\setminus T
(i.e., not corresponding to an active type) are required to be zero by the definition of the
\bar{x}
-polytope.

For the following, fix a group
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
. A
movement
of
G
is a vector
\bar{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{I\times(I\cup{\mathcal{C}})}
which is used as a relaxation of the constraints corresponding to items in
G
in the
\bar{x}
-polytope:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{m}_{\ell,t}=0~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\forall\ell\in I,t\in T\text{ s.t. }\ell\notin G\textnormal{ or }\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}\in\{0,1\}~

(33)

\displaystyle\rule{0.0pt}{18.00005pt}\displaystyle\sum_{t\in T}\bar{m}_{\ell,t}=0~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\forall\ell\in I~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

(34)

\displaystyle\rule{0.0pt}{18.00005pt}\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{m}_{\ell,t}=0~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\forall t\in T\text{ s.t.}\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=L_{t,G}~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

(35)

We say that
G
is
fractional
if there are
\ell\in G
and
t\in T
such that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}\in(0,1)
.

Claim B.1
.

For every group
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
, If
G
is fractional then
G
has a movement
\bar{m}\neq 0
.

The proof of Claim
B.1
utilizes properties of
totally unimodular
matrices. A matrix
A
is totally unimodular if every square submatrix of
A
has a determinant
1
,
-1
or
0
. If
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
is totally unimodular and
b\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
is an integral vector, it holds that the vertices of the polytope

P_{A}=\{\bar{v}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}~|~A\bar{v}\leq\bar{b}\}

are integral. That is, if
\bar{v}\in P_{A}
is a vertex of
P_{A}
then
\bar{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}

[
28
]
. We use the following criteria for total unimodularity, which is a simplified version of a theorem from
[
28
]
.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
be a matrix which satisfies the following properties.

•

All the entries of
A
are in
\{-1,1,0\}
.

•

Every column of
A
has up to two non-zero entries.

•

If a column of
A
has two non-zero entries, then these entries have opposite signs.

Then,
A
is totally unimodular.

Proof of Claim
B.1
:

Let
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
be a fractional group. Define
X=\{(\ell,t)\in G\times T~|~\ell\notin\textsf{fit}(t)\}
as all
forbidden pairs
of
G
, where for all
(\ell,t)\in X
it holds that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=0
by (
4
). We show the existence of the movement
\bar{m}\neq 0
using the polytope
P
defined as follows.

P=\left\{{\bar{y}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{G\times T}~\middle|~\begin{array}[]{lcc}\displaystyle\forall(\ell,t)\in X&:&\displaystyle{\bar{y}}_{\ell,t}\leq 0\\
\rule{0.0pt}{18.00005pt}\displaystyle\forall\ell\in G&:&\displaystyle\sum_{t\in T\textnormal{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in\notin X}-{\bar{y}}_{\ell,t}\leq-1\\
\rule{0.0pt}{18.00005pt}\displaystyle\forall t\in T&:&\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G\textnormal{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\notin X}{\bar{y}}_{\ell,t}\leq L_{t,G}\end{array}\right\}.

(36)

Define a vector
\bar{\phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{G\times T}
by
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}
for all
(\ell,t)\in G\times T
. It follows from the definition of the
\bar{x}
-polytope that
\bar{\phi}\in P
. We can represent the inequalities in (
36
) using a matrix notation as

P=\left\{{\bar{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{G\times T}_{\geq 0}~\middle|~A{\bar{y}}\leq\bar{b}\right\}.

It follows that
A
contains only entries in
\{-1,0,1\}
and the entries in
\bar{b}
are all integral. Furthermore, every column of
A
contains at most
2
non-zero entries, and if there are two non-zero entries in a column then they are of a different sign. By Lemma
B.2
, it follows that
A
is totally unimodular. It thus holds that all the vertices of the polytope
P
are integral. As
\bar{\phi}\in P
is non-integral (since
G
is fractional), it follows that
\bar{\phi}
is not a vertex of
P
. Hence, there is a vector
\bar{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{G\times T}
,
\bar{\gamma}\neq 0
such that
\bar{\phi}+\bar{\gamma},\bar{\phi}-\bar{\gamma}\in P
.

We define
\bar{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{I\times(I\cup{\mathcal{C}})}
by
\bar{m}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}
for all
(\ell,t)\in G\times T
and
\bar{m}_{\ell,t}=0
otherwise. Clearly,
\bar{m}\neq 0
as
\bar{\gamma}\neq 0
. Observe that for
\ell\in I\setminus G
and
t\in(I\cup{\mathcal{C}})
it holds that
\bar{m}_{\ell,t}=0
by definition. For
\ell\in G
and
t\in(I\cup{\mathcal{C}})
such that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=0
, as
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t},\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}-\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}\geq 0
and
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=0
, it follows that
\bar{m}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=0
.
For
\ell\in G
and
t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=1
, for all
t^{\prime}\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\{t\}
it holds that
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t^{\prime}}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t^{\prime}}=0
because constraint (
8
) in the
\bar{x}
-polytope is tight for
\bar{\lambda}
. Thus, by the previous argument, we have
\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t^{\prime}}=0
for every
t^{\prime}\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\{t\}
in case that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=1
. Therefore,

-1-\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=-\sum_{t^{\prime}\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}\textnormal{ s.t }(\ell,t^{\prime})\notin X}\left(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t^{\prime}}+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t^{\prime}}\right)\leq-1.

(37)

The equality is because
\bar{\lambda}
and also
\bar{\phi}
hold constraint (
8
) with equality. The last inequality is due to
\bar{\phi}+\bar{\gamma}\in P
.
Similarly, as
\bar{\phi}-\bar{\gamma}\in P
, we have

-1+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=-\sum_{t^{\prime}\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}\textnormal{ s.t }(\ell,t^{\prime})\notin X}\left(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t^{\prime}}-\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t^{\prime}}\right)\leq-1.

(38)

By (
37
) and (
38
) we have
\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=0
. Overall, we have that
\bar{m}
satisfies (
33
).

For any
\ell\in I\setminus G
it holds that
\sum_{t\in T}\bar{m}_{\ell,t}=0
by (
33
). For
\ell\in G
, since
\bar{\phi}+\bar{\gamma}\in P
we have

-1-\sum_{t\in T}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{t\in T}-(\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t})=\sum_{t\in T~\textnormal{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\notin X}-(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t})\leq-1,

(39)

where the first equality holds since
\bar{\lambda}
satisfies with equality constraint (
8
) of the
\bar{x}
-polytope and the second equality uses
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=0
for
(\ell,t)\in X
because
\phi+\bar{\gamma}\in P
. Similarly, since
\phi-\bar{\gamma}\in P
, we have

-1+\sum_{t\in T}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{t\in T}-(\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}-\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t})=\sum_{t\in T~\textnormal{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\notin X}-(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}-\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t})\leq-1.

(40)

By (
39
) and (
40
) we have

\sum_{t\in T}\bar{m}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{t\in T}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=0
. Thus,
\bar{m}
satisfies (
34
). Finally, let
t\in T
such that
\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=L_{t,G}
. As before,

L_{t,G}+\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}+\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{\ell\in G}\left(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}\right)=\sum_{\ell\in G\textnormal{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\notin X}\left(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}\right)\leq L_{t,G},

(41)

where the inequality follows from
\bar{\phi}+\bar{\gamma}\in P
. Using a similar argument,

L_{t,G}-\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}-\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{\ell\in G}\left(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}-\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}\right)=\sum_{\ell\in G\textnormal{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\notin X}\left(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}-\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}\right)\leq L_{t,G}.

(42)

By (
41
) and (
42
), we have
\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{m}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=0
. Thus,
\bar{m}
satisfies (
35
). Overall, we show that
\bar{m}\neq 0
is a movement of
G
.
∎

Claim B.3
.

There are at most
|T|
fractional groups.

Proof.

Assume towards a contradiction that there are
|T|+1
fractional groups. Denote these groups by
G_{1},\ldots,G_{|T|+1}\in{\mathcal{G}}
. By Lemma
B.1
, for all
j\in[|T|+1]
it holds that
G_{j}
has a movement
\bar{m}^{j}\neq 0
. Consider the following set of equalities over
a_{1},\ldots,a_{|T|+1}
:

\forall t\in T:~~~~\sum_{j=1}^{|T|+1}a_{j}\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{m}^{j}_{\ell,t}\cdot s(\ell)=0.

(43)

These are
|T|
homogeneous linear equalities in
|T|+1
variables. Thus, there exist
a_{1},\ldots,a_{|T|+1}\in\mathbb{R}
, not all zeros, for which (
43
) holds. Define

K_{1}=\min_{(\ell,t)\in I\times T}\min\left\{\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t},1-\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}~\middle|~0<\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}<1\right\},

K_{2}=\min\left\{L_{t,G_{j}}-\sum_{\ell\in G_{j}}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}~\middle|~j\in[|T|+1],t\in T\text{ s.t. }\sum_{\ell\in G_{j}}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}<L_{t,G_{j}}\right\},

and
K=\min\{K_{1},K_{2}\}
. Additionally, define

W=\max\left\{|\bar{m}^{j}_{\ell,t}|~|~1\leq j\leq|T|+1,~(\ell,t)\in I\times T\right\}.

Observe that
W,K>0
. Using a scaling argument, we may assume that for any
1\leq j\leq|T|+1
it holds that

a_{j}\leq\frac{K}{|I|\cdot W}.

Define

\bar{\phi}=\bar{\lambda}+\sum_{j=1}^{|T|+1}a_{j}\cdot\bar{m}^{j}.

In the following we show that
\bar{\phi}
is in the
\bar{x}
-polytope. For every
\ell\in I\setminus(G_{1}\cup\ldots\cup G_{|T|+1})
and
t\in T
it holds that
\bar{m}^{1}_{\ell,t}=\ldots=\bar{m}^{|T|+1}_{\ell,t}=0
due to (
33
). Also, for all
\ell\in I
and
t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}\setminus T
, it holds that
\bar{m}^{1}_{\ell,t}=\ldots=\bar{m}^{|T|+1}_{\ell,t}=0
due to (
33
) as
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=0
. Thus,

\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}\in[0,1]
in this case. Also, for
\ell\in I
and
t\in T
such that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}\in\{0,1\}
it holds that
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=0
by (
33
). Finally, for
j\in[|T|+1]
,
\ell\in G_{j}
, and
t\in T
such that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}\in(0,1)
, by (
33
) it holds that
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}+a_{j}\cdot\bar{m}^{j}_{\ell,t}
. Following the definitions of
K
and
W
, we have

0\leq K-W\cdot\frac{K}{|I|\cdot W}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}+a_{j}\cdot\bar{m}^{j}_{\ell,t}\leq 1-K+W\cdot\frac{K}{|I|\cdot W}\leq 1.

Thus,
\bar{\phi}\in[0,1]^{I\times(I\cup{\mathcal{C}})}
.

For every
(\ell,t)\in I\times T
such that
\ell\notin\textsf{fit}(t)
, it holds that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=0
by (
4
) since
\bar{\lambda}
is in the
\bar{x}
-polytope. Thus by (
33
) we have
\bar{m}^{j}_{\ell,t}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq|T|+1
. Therefore,
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}=0
. We conclude that constraint (
4
) is satisfied for
\bar{\phi}
.

For every
t\in{\mathcal{C}}
it holds that

\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}\cdot s(\ell)=\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}\cdot s(\ell)+\sum_{j=1}^{|T|+1}a_{j}\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{m}^{j}_{\ell,t}s(\ell)=\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}\cdot s(\ell)\leq(1-s(t))\bar{x}_{t}.

where the second equality is by (
43
), and the inequality is due to
\bar{\lambda}
is in the
\bar{x}
-polytope; hence, the inequality follows by (
5
).

Let
G\in\mathcal{G}
and
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
. If for all
1\leq j\leq|T|+1
it holds that
G\neq G_{j}
, then
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,C}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}
for every
\ell\in G
by (
33
). Thus,

\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,C}=\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}\leq\bar{x}_{C}\cdot(k(G)-|G\cap C|)
, as
\bar{\lambda}
is in the
\bar{x}
-polytope and thus satisfies constraint (
6
). Otherwise, there is
1\leq j\leq|T|+1
such that
G=G_{j}
. Observe that for every
\ell\in G
it holds that
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,C}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}+a_{j}\bar{m}^{j}_{\ell,C}
by (
33
);
consider the following cases.

•

\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}<L_{C,G}
. Using the definitions of
K
and
W
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,C}=\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}+a_{j}\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{m}^{j}_{\ell,C}\leq L_{C,G}-K+\frac{K}{|I|\cdot W}\cdot|I|\cdot W=L_{C,G}=\bar{x}_{C}\cdot(k(G)-|G\cap C|).

•

\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}=L_{C,G}
. By (
35
) we have

\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,C}=\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}+a_{j}\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{m}^{j}_{\ell,C}=L_{C,G}=\bar{x}_{C}\cdot(k(G)-|G\cap C|).

(44)

We showed that
\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,C}\leq\bar{x}_{C}
in all cases. We conclude that constraint (
6
) is satisfied for
\bar{\phi}
.

Let
j\in I
. We split into two cases, as follows.

1.

For all
1\leq i\leq|T|+1
it holds that
\textsf{group}(j)\neq G_{i}
. Then, by (
2
), for all
\ell\notin\textsf{group}(j)
it holds that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}=0
; thus, it holds that
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,j}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}
for every
\ell\in I
by (
33
). By the above,
\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,j}=\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}\leq\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[j]}\bar{x}_{C}
, as
\bar{\lambda}
is in the
\bar{x}
-polytope and thus satisfies constraint (
7
).

2.

There is
1\leq i\leq|T|+1
such that
j\in G_{i}
. Observe that for every
\ell\in I
it holds that
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,j}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}+a_{i}\bar{m}^{i}_{\ell,j}
by (
33
) since for items
\ell\notin G_{i}
it holds that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}=0
;
consider the following cases.

•

\sum_{\ell\in G_{i}}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}<L_{j,G_{i}}
. Using the definitions of
K
and
W
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,j}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}+a_{i}\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{m}^{i}_{\ell,j}=\sum_{\ell\in G_{i}}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}+a_{i}\sum_{\ell\in G_{i}}\bar{m}^{i}_{\ell,j}\leq L_{j,G_{i}}-K+\frac{K}{|I|\cdot W}\cdot|I|\cdot W

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle L_{j,G_{i}}=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[j]}\bar{x}_{C}.

•

\sum_{\ell\in G_{i}}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}=L_{j,G_{i}}
. By (
35
) we have

\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,j}=\sum_{\ell\in G_{i}}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,j}=\sum_{\ell\in G_{i}}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}+a_{i}\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{m}^{i}_{\ell,j}=L_{j,G}=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[j]}\bar{x}_{C}.

(45)

We showed that
\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,j}\leq L_{j,G}
in all cases. We conclude that constraint (
7
) is satisfied for
\bar{\phi}
.

For every
\ell\in I
, we have

\sum_{t\in T}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{t\in T}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}+\sum_{j=1}^{|T|+1}a_{j}\sum_{t\in T}\bar{m}^{j}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{t\in T}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}\geq 1,

where the second equality is by (
34
), and the last inequality follows since
\bar{\lambda}
is in the
\bar{x}
-polytope; thus, it satisfies constraint (
8
). Overall, we have that
\bar{\phi}
is in the
\bar{x}
-polytope.

We can also define
\bar{\phi}^{\prime}=\bar{\lambda}-\sum_{j=1}^{|T|+1}a_{j}\cdot\bar{m}^{j}
. By a symmetric argument we can show that
\bar{\phi}^{\prime}
is in the
\bar{x}
-polytope as well. It also holds that
\bar{\phi},\bar{\phi}^{\prime}\neq\bar{\lambda}
as there is
1\leq j^{*}\leq|T|+1
such that
a_{j^{*}}\neq 0
; also, there are
\ell^{*}\in G_{j^{*}}
and
t^{*}\in T
such that
\bar{m}^{j^{*}}_{\ell^{*},t^{*}}\neq 0
. Thus,
\bar{\phi}_{\ell^{*},j^{*}}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell^{*},t^{*}}+a_{j^{*}}\bar{m}^{j^{*}}_{\ell^{*},j^{*}}\neq\bar{\lambda}_{\ell^{*},t^{*}}
and
\bar{\phi}^{\prime}_{\ell^{*},j^{*}}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell^{*},t^{*}}-a_{j^{*}}\bar{m}^{j^{*}}_{\ell^{*},j^{*}}\neq\bar{\lambda}_{\ell^{*},t^{*}}
.
Furthermore,
\bar{\lambda}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\bar{\phi}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\bar{\phi}^{\prime}
, and we conclude that
\bar{\lambda}
is not a vertex of the
\bar{x}
-polytope. Contradiction.
∎

Lemma B.4
.

If a group
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
is fractional, then

|\{\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}\in(0,1)~|~\ell\in G,t\in T\}|\leq 2|T|.

Proof.

Let
X=\{(\ell,t)\in G\times T~|~\ell\notin\textsf{fit}(t)\}
. Define
R=G\times T\setminus X
as the set of all pairs of items where the first fits in the place of the second.
Let
Q\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{R}
be the set (polytope) of all the vectors
\bar{\rho}\in\mathbb{R}^{R}
which satisfy the following constraints:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{\ell,t}\geq 0~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\forall(\ell,t)\in R~~~~~~~

(46)

\displaystyle\rule{0.0pt}{18.00005pt}\displaystyle\sum_{t\in T\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\rho}_{\ell,t}\geq 1~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\forall\ell\in G~~~~~~~~~~~~

(47)

\displaystyle\rule{0.0pt}{18.00005pt}\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\rho}_{\ell,C}\cdot s(\ell)+\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}\cdot s(\ell)\leq(1-s(C))\bar{x}_{C}~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\forall C\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap T~~~~~~

(48)

\displaystyle\rule{0.0pt}{18.00005pt}\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\rho}_{\ell,t}\leq L_{t,G}~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\forall t\in T~~~~~~~~~~~~

(49)

Now, we define
\bar{\phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{R}
by
\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}
for all
(\ell,t)\in R
. As
\bar{\lambda}
belongs to the
\bar{x}
-polytope,
it easily follows that
\bar{\phi}\in Q
since
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=0
for all
(\ell,t)\in(I\times I\cup{\mathcal{C}})\setminus R
by (
4
) (i.e., where
\ell\notin\textsf{fit}(t)
).

Claim B.5
.

\bar{\phi}
is a vertex of
Q
.

Proof.

Assume towards contradiction that
\bar{\phi}
is not a vertex of
Q
. Thus, there is a vector
\bar{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{R}
,
\bar{\gamma}\neq 0
such that
\bar{\phi}+\bar{\gamma},\bar{\phi}-\bar{\gamma}\in Q
. Thus, by (
47
), for all
\ell\in G
we get that

1\leq\sum_{t\in T\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t})=\sum_{t\in T\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}+\sum_{t\in T\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=1+\sum_{t\in T\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}

The last equality is because
\bar{\lambda}
satisfies constraint (
8
) of the
\bar{x}
-polytope with equality and by the definition of
\phi
. In addition,

1\leq\sum_{t\in T\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}-\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t})=\sum_{t\in T\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}-\sum_{t\in T\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=1-\sum_{t\in T\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}

The last equality is because
\bar{\lambda}
satisfies constraint (
8
) of the
\bar{x}
-polytope with equality and by the definition of
\phi
. Hence, by the above it follows that

\sum_{t\in T\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=0.

(50)

Furthermore, for all
t\in T
the following hold by (
49
):

L_{t,G}\geq\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t})=\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}+\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}

and

L_{t,G}\geq\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}-\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t})=\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}-\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}

Hence, by the above, because for all
(\ell,t)\in R
it holds that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}
, and that by (
4
) for all
(\ell,t)\notin R
it holds that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=0
, for all
t\in T
it follows that:

\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}-L_{t,G}\leq\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}\leq L_{t,G}-\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}.

(51)

Define
\bar{\lambda}^{+},\bar{\lambda}^{-}\in\mathbb{R}^{I\times(I\cup\mathcal{C})}
by
\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\lambda}^{-}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}
for
(\ell,t)\in\left(I\times(I\cup\mathcal{C})\right)\setminus R
, and
\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t},~\bar{\lambda}^{-}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}-\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}
for
(\ell,t)\in R
. Since
\bar{\gamma}\neq 0
it follows that
\bar{\lambda}^{+}\neq\bar{\lambda}^{-}
.

We now show that
\bar{\lambda}^{+},\bar{\lambda}^{-}
are in the
\bar{x}
-polytope. Let
\ell\in I,t\in I\cup\mathcal{C}
such that
\ell\notin\textsf{fit}(t)
. Therefore,
(\ell,t)\notin R
; thus,
\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,t}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=0
because
\bar{\lambda}
is in the
\bar{x}
-polytope. We conclude that (
4
) is satisfied for
\bar{\lambda}^{+}
. Let
C\in\mathcal{C}
. Then,

\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,C}\cdot s(\ell)=\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}(\bar{\phi}_{\ell,C}+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C})\cdot s(\ell)+\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}\cdot s(\ell)\leq\left(1-s(C)\right)\cdot\bar{x}_{C}.

(52)

The inequality is because
\bar{\phi}+\bar{\gamma}\in Q
. By (
52
) we conclude that (
5
) is satisfied for
\bar{\lambda}^{+}
. Let
G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
and
C\in\mathcal{C}
. Then, if
G^{\prime}\neq G
it holds that:

\sum_{\ell\in G^{\prime}}\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,C}=\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}\leq\bar{x}_{C}\cdot(k(G)-|G\cap C|)=L_{C,G}.

(53)

The inequality is because
\bar{\lambda}
is in the
\bar{x}
-polytope and satisfies (
6
). Otherwise,
G^{\prime}=G
.

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,C}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}+\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}=\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}+\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }(\ell,C)\in R}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}

(54)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}+\left(L_{C,G}-\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}\right)=L_{C,G}=\bar{x}_{C}\cdot(k(G)-|G\cap C|).

The inequality is by (
51
). By (
53
) and (
54
) we conclude that (
6
) is satisfied for
\bar{\lambda}^{+}
.

Let
j\in I
. We split into two cases.

1.

j\notin G
. Then, by (
2
) and (
4
) for all
\ell\notin\textsf{group}(j)
it holds that
\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,j}=0
and for all
\ell^{\prime}\in I
it holds that
\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell^{\prime},j}=\bar{\lambda}_{\ell^{\prime},j}
. Therefore,

\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,j}=\sum_{\ell\in\textsf{group}(j)}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}^{+}=\sum_{\ell\in\textsf{group}(j)}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}\leq\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[j]}\bar{x}_{C}.

2.

j\in G
. Then, by (
2
) and (
4
) for all
\ell\notin G
it holds that
\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,j}=0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,j}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,j}=\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}+\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }(\ell,j)\in R}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}+\left(L_{j,G}-\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}\right)=L_{j,G}=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[j]}\bar{x}_{C}.

The inequality is by (
51
).

By the above, we conclude that (
7
) is satisfied for
\bar{\lambda}^{+}
.

Let
\ell\in I
. We split into two cases.

1.

\ell\notin G
.

\sum_{t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}\geq 1.

The last inequality is because
\bar{\lambda}
satisfies constraint (
8
) of the
\bar{x}
-polytope.

2.

\ell\in G
.

\sum_{t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\lambda}^{+}_{\ell,t}\geq\sum_{t\in T}\left(\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}+\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}\right)=\sum_{t\in T}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}+\sum_{t\in T\text{ s.t. }(\ell,t)\in R}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{t\in T}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}\geq 1.

The second equality is by (
50
).

By the above, constraint (
8
) is satisfied for
\bar{\lambda}^{+}
. Overall, we conclude that
\bar{\lambda}^{+}
is in the
\bar{x}
-polytope.

Using a symmetric argument it follows that
\bar{\lambda}^{-}
is also in the
\bar{x}
-polytope. Since
\bar{\lambda}=\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{+}+\bar{\lambda}^{-}}{2}
, it follows that
\bar{\lambda}
is not a vertex of the
\bar{x}
-polytope. Contradiction. Thus,
\bar{\phi}
is a vertex of
Q
.
∎

Let
F=\{\ell\in G~|~\exists t\in T\text{ s.t. }\bar{\phi}_{\ell,t}\in(0,1)\}
. By the definition of
\bar{\phi}
, it suffices to show that
|F|\leq 2|T|
.
For any
\ell\in I
define
\delta_{\ell}=|\{t\in T~|~(\ell,t)\in R\}|
to be all of the corresponding pairs of
\ell
in
R
. It follows that
|R|=\sum_{\ell\in I}\delta_{\ell}
. For
\ell\in F
, up to
\delta_{\ell}-1
of the corresponding inequalities of
\ell
in (
46
) and (
47
) are tight: to satisfy (
47
) for
\ell
at least two inequalities corresponding to
\ell
in (
46
) are not tight. In addition, for
\ell\in G\setminus F
, up to
\delta_{\ell}
corresponding constraints in (
46
) and (
47
) for
\ell
are tight: to satisfy (
47
) for
\ell
at least one inequality corresponding to
\ell
in (
46
) is not tight. Moreover, there are at most
2|T|
constraints in (
48
) and (
49
) that can be tight. Hence, the number of tight constraints is at most

\sum_{\ell\in F}(\delta_{\ell}-1)+\sum_{\ell\in G\setminus F}\delta_{\ell}+2|T|\leq|R|-|F|+2|T|.

(55)

As
\bar{\phi}
is a vertex of
Q
, there are at least
|R|
tight inequalities. Thus,
|F|\leq 2|T|
. Note that for all
t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}\setminus T
and
\ell\in I
it holds that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=0
by the constraints of the
\bar{x}
-polytope; thus, such entries cannot be fractional, and the number of fractional entries corresponding to items in
G
is at most
2|T|
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.4
:

Note that for all
t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}\setminus T
and
\ell\in I
it holds that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=0
by the constraints of the
\bar{x}
-polytope; thus, such entries cannot be non-integral. In addition, by Lemma
B.3
and Lemma
B.4
the number of non-integral entries is at most
2|T|\cdot|T|
. Therefore,

2|T|\cdot|T|\leq 2\left(|\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})|+k\cdot|\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})|\right)^{2}\leq 2\left(2k\cdot|\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})|\right)^{2}=8k^{2}|\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})|^{2}.

The first inequality is because
|I\cap T|
is upper bounded by the size of
\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})
times the number of items in each configuration in
\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})
. ∎

Appendix C
Omitted Proofs of Section
4

Proof of Lemma
4.1
:

Let
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r}
be the items in
C\setminus L
in decreasing order such that for all
a,b\in[r],a<b
it holds that
\ell_{a}<\ell_{b}
. In addition, let
U_{C}~=~\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{h}\}
such that
h=\alpha
. For the simplicity of the notations, for any
j\in[h]
let
T_{j}=s\left(C\cap L\cup\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{j-1}\}\right)
be the total size of the large items in
C
and the first
j-1
small items in
C
according to the above sorted order of items.
8
8

8

With a slight abuse of notation, assume that
\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{j-1}\}=\emptyset
for
j=1
.
We use the following auxiliary claims.

Claim C.1
.

For all
i,k\in[h]
which satisfy that
k>i+{\varepsilon}^{-4}
, it holds that
s(\ell_{i})>\frac{s(\ell_{k})}{{\varepsilon}^{2}}
.

Proof.

Assume towards a contradiction that there are
i,k\in[h]
which satisfy that
k>i+{\varepsilon}^{-4}
and
s(\ell_{i})\leq\frac{s(\ell_{k})}{{\varepsilon}^{2}}
. Thus,

s(\{\ell_{i},\ell_{i+1},\ldots,\ell_{k}\})\geq(k-i)\cdot s(\ell_{k})\geq{\varepsilon}^{-4}\cdot s(\ell_{k})\geq{\varepsilon}^{-4}\cdot{\varepsilon}^{2}\cdot s(\ell_{i})={\varepsilon}^{-2}s(\ell_{i})

(56)

The first inequality is because the items are in decreasing order that induces a non increasing order of item sizes. The second inequality is because
k>i+{\varepsilon}^{-4}
. The third inequality is by the assumption that
s(\ell_{i})\leq\frac{s(\ell_{k})}{{\varepsilon}^{2}}
.

Note that it holds that
T_{i}<1
since
T_{i}
is the total size of items in a proper subset of
C
and the sizes of the items are strictly larger than zero. Now, because
\ell_{i}\in U_{C}
and
i<\alpha
, we conclude by (
3
) and (
9
) that

s(\ell_{i})>{\varepsilon}\left(1-T_{i}\right).

(57)

Therefore,

s(C)\geq T_{i}+s(\{\ell_{i},\ldots,\ell_{k}\})\geq T_{i}+{\varepsilon}^{-1}\left(1-T_{i}\right)\geq T_{i}+\left(1-T_{i}\right)+({\varepsilon}^{-1}-1)(1-T_{i})>1.

(58)

The first inequality is because
C\cap L\cup\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{i-1}\}
is disjoint to
\{\ell_{i},\ldots,\ell_{k}\}
. The second inequality is by (
56
) and (
57
). The last inequality follows since
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
and
0\leq T_{i}<1
. By (
58
), we reach a contradiction to the fact that
s(C)\leq 1
because
C
is a configuration.
∎

Claim C.2
.

For any
i\in[h]
such that
i+{\varepsilon}^{-4}<h-1
it holds that
\ell_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{C}
.

Proof.

s(\ell_{i})>\frac{s(\ell_{h-1})}{{\varepsilon}^{2}}>{\varepsilon}^{-2}\cdot{\varepsilon}(1-T_{h-1})={\varepsilon}^{-1}(1-T_{h-1})\geq{\varepsilon}^{-1}(1-s(R(C))).

(59)

The first inequality is by Claim
C.1
. The second inequality is because
h-1<h\leq\alpha
; thus, similarly to (
57
), it holds that
s(\ell_{h-1})>{\varepsilon}\left(1-T_{h-1}\right)
. The last inequality is because
T_{h-1}
is the total size in a proper subset of
R(C)
; thus,
T_{h-1}\leq s(R(C))
. By (
59
), we conclude that for any
i\in[h]
such that
i+{\varepsilon}^{-4}<h-1
it holds that
\ell_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{C}
.
∎

Now, using the above claim:

|\mathcal{L}_{C}|\geq h-2-{\varepsilon}^{-4}\geq{\varepsilon}^{-5}-2{\varepsilon}^{-4}={\varepsilon}^{-4}({\varepsilon}^{-1}-2)>{\varepsilon}^{-4}.

The first inequality is since by Claim
C.2
it holds that
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{t}\in\mathcal{L}_{C}
for
t=h-2-{\varepsilon}^{-4}
. The second inequality is because
h=\alpha
. ∎

Proof of Lemma
4.2
:

Let
C\in\mathcal{C}
. We split the proof into the following claims which together form the proof of the lemma.

Claim C.3
.

Condition 1 of Lemma
4.2
holds for
|U_{C}|=\alpha
.

Proof.

\|\bar{w}^{C}\|=\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{C}}\bar{w}^{C}_{R(C)\setminus\{\ell\}}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{C}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{L}_{C}|-1}=\frac{|\mathcal{L}_{C}|}{|\mathcal{L}_{C}|-1}\leq\frac{{\varepsilon}^{-4}}{{\varepsilon}^{-4}-1}\leq 1+2{\varepsilon}^{4}.

The first equality is because by the definition
\bar{w}^{C}
, for any other entry
C^{\prime}
that is not in the second summation it holds that
\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}=0
. The second equality is by the definition of
\bar{w}^{C}
in case that
|U_{C}|=\alpha
. The first inequality is because
|U_{C}|=\alpha
; therefore, by Lemma
4.1
it holds that
|\mathcal{L}_{C}|\geq{\varepsilon}^{-4}
. The last inequality is because
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
.
∎

Claim C.4
.

Condition 2 of Lemma
4.2
holds for
|U_{C}|=\alpha
.

Proof.

Let
\ell\in R(C)
. Then,

\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]}\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}\geq\sum_{j\in\mathcal{L}_{C}\setminus\{\ell\}}\bar{w}^{C}_{R(C)\setminus\{j\}}=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{L}_{C}\setminus\{\ell\}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{L}_{C}|-1}\geq\frac{|\mathcal{L}_{C}|-1}{|\mathcal{L}_{C}|-1}=1.

The first inequality is since for any
j\in\mathcal{L}_{C}\setminus\{\ell\}
it holds that
R(C)\setminus\{j\}\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]
and that all entries in
\bar{w}^{C}
are non-negative. The first equality is by the definition of
\bar{w}^{C}
in case that
|U_{C}|=\alpha
. The last inequality is because
|\mathcal{L}_{C}\setminus\{\ell\}|\geq|\mathcal{L}_{C}|-1
.
∎

Claim C.5
.

Condition 3 of Lemma
4.2
holds for
|U_{C}|=\alpha
.

Proof.

For the third condition of the lemma, let
C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{w}^{C})
. By the definition of
\bar{w}^{C}
in case that
|U_{C}|=\alpha
, there is
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{C}
such that
C^{\prime}=R(C)\setminus\{\ell\}
. Now,

1-s(R(C))=1-s(R(C)\setminus\{\ell\})-s(\ell)\leq 1-s(R(C)\setminus\{\ell\})-\frac{1-s(R(C))}{{\varepsilon}}.

(60)

The first equality is because
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{C}
and
\mathcal{L}_{C}\subseteq R(C)
. The first inequality is by the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{C}
. Therefore,

1-s(R(C))\leq\left(\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}}\right)(1-s(R(C)\setminus\{\ell\}))\leq{\varepsilon}(1-s(R(C)\setminus\{\ell\})).

(61)

The first inequality is by (
60
). Let
j\in C\setminus R(C)
. Now,

s(j)\leq 1-s(R(C))\leq{\varepsilon}(1-s(R(C)\setminus\{\ell\})).

(62)

The first inequality is because
j\notin R(C)
and
s(C)\leq 1
. The second inequality is by (
61
). Therefore, by (
62
) we get that
j\in\textsf{fit}(C^{\prime})
by (
3
) (because
j\in C\setminus R(C)
it holds that
j\notin L
). Because there are no restrictions over
j
besides that
j\in C\setminus R(C)
, we conclude that
C\setminus R(C)\subseteq\textsf{fit}(C^{\prime})
. In addition,

s(C\setminus R(C))\leq 1-s(R(C))\leq 1-s(R(C)\setminus\{\ell\})=1-s(C^{\prime}).

The first inequality is because
R(C)\subseteq C
and
s(C)\leq 1
. The second inequality is because
s(\ell)\geq 0
. The first equality is by the definition of
C^{\prime}
.
∎

Claim C.6
.

Condition 4 of Lemma
4.2
holds.

Proof.

let
C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{w}^{C})
. Assume towards a contradiction that
s(C^{\prime}\setminus L)>{\varepsilon}
. Therefore,

{\varepsilon}<s(C^{\prime}\setminus L)\leq 1-s(C^{\prime}\cap L).

(63)

The second inequality is because
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}
is a configuration. Recall
\ell_{1}
from (
9
) for
C^{\prime}
; it also holds that

s(\ell_{1})\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}={\varepsilon}\cdot{\varepsilon}<{\varepsilon}\cdot\left(1-s(C^{\prime}\cap L)\right).

(64)

The first inequality is because
\ell_{1}\in I\setminus L
. The last inequality is by (
63
). Therefore, by (
64
) we get that
\ell_{1}\in\textsf{fit}(C^{\prime}\cap L)
by (
3
). By the definition of the relaxation
\bar{w}^{C}
it holds that
C^{\prime}\cap L=C\cap L
, thus
\ell_{1}\in\textsf{fit}(C\cap L)
. Then, by (
9
) and the definition of
U_{C}
we conclude that
U_{C}=\emptyset
. Hence, by the definition of
\bar{w}^{C}
and that
C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{w}^{C})
, it holds that
C^{\prime}=C\cap L
; thus,
s(C^{\prime}\setminus L)=0
. For any
{\varepsilon}>0
, this is a contradiction that
s(C^{\prime}\setminus L)>{\varepsilon}
.
∎

Claim C.7
.

Condition 5 of Lemma
4.2
holds.

Proof.

Let
C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{w}^{C})
and
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
. Now,

\left|G\cap\left(C\setminus R(C)\right)\right|\leq|G\cap C|-|G\cap R(C)|\leq k(G)-|G\cap R(C)|\leq k(G)-|G\cap C^{\prime}|.

The last inequality is because by the definition of
\bar{w}^{C}
it holds that
C^{\prime}\subseteq R(C)

∎

Claim C.8
.

Conditions 1-3 of Lemma
4.2
hold for
|U_{C}|<\alpha
.

Proof.

For the first condition of the lemma:

\|\bar{w}^{C}\|=\bar{w}^{C}_{R(C)}=1\leq 1+2{\varepsilon}^{4}.

The first equality is because by the definition
\bar{w}^{C}
, for any other configuration
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\{R(C)\}
it holds that
\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}=0
. The second equality is by the definition of
\bar{w}^{C}
in case that
|U_{C}|<\alpha
.

For the second condition of the lemma, let
\ell\in R(C)
. Then,

\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]}\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}=\bar{w}^{C}_{R(C)}=1.

The first equality is because by the definition
\bar{w}^{C}
, for any other configuration
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\{R(C)\}
it holds that
\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}=0
. The second equality is bythe definition of
\bar{w}^{C}
in case that
|U_{C}|<\alpha
.

For the third condition of the lemma, let
C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{w}^{C})
. By the definition of
\bar{w}^{C}
in case that
|U_{C}|<\alpha
, it holds that
C^{\prime}=R(C)
. Let
j\in C\setminus R(C)
. It holds that
j\in\textsf{fit}(C^{\prime})
by (
9
), since

. Because there are no restrictions over

besides that

, we conclude that

. In addition,

s(C\setminus R(C))\leq 1-s(R(C))=1-s(C^{\prime}).

The first inequality is because
R(C)\subseteq C
and
s(C)\leq 1
. The first equality is by the definition of

.

follows by Claim

, Claim

, Claim

, Claim

, and Claim
