.

Proof of Lemma
3.6
:

We use in the proof of Lemma
3.6
the following claims.

Claim C.9
.

Algorithm
2
is polynomial.

Proof.

The number of elements over which the loop in Step
2
iterates is polynomial, since
|\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})|
is at most the encoding size of the input (in a sparse representation). Let
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})
be a configuration in the support of
\bar{x}
. It follows by (
9
) that
U_{C}
can be easily computed in polynomial time by iteratively adding items from
C\setminus L
in decreasing order of the items. Therefore, given
U_{C}
, the relaxation of
C
that is
\bar{w}^{C}
can be computed in polynomial time: by the definition of
\bar{w}^{C}
, the number of nonzero entries in
\bar{w}^{C}
is at most
|\mathcal{L}_{C}|
, which is polynomial in the size of the instance. This is since
\mathcal{L}_{C}\subseteq I
is a subset of the items. Finally, Step
2
is also polynomial as
\bar{y}
is a linear combination of a polynomial number (i.e.,
|\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})|
) of vectors, each with a polynomial number of nonzero entries. By the above, the claim follows.
∎

Claim C.10
.

For any
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
, an
{\varepsilon}
-structured BPP instance
\mathcal{I}
, and a solution
\bar{x}
to the configuration LP in (
1
), Algorithm
2
returns a prototype
\bar{y}
of
\mathcal{I}
with
\bar{\lambda}
in the
\bar{y}
-polytope such that for all
\ell,j\in I,\ell\neq j
it holds that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}=0
.

Proof.

We define below a point
\bar{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{I\times(I\cup\mathcal{C})}_{\geq 0}
in the
\bar{y}
-polytope. Thus, it follows that the
\bar{y}
-polytope is non-empty. For any
C\in\mathcal{C}
, define the
point of
C
as a vector
\bar{\gamma}^{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{I\times(I\cup\mathcal{C})}_{\geq 0}
as follows. For any
\ell\in R(C)
, define

\bar{\gamma}^{C}_{\ell,\ell}=1.

(65)

Moreover, for any
\ell\in C\setminus R(C)
and
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
define

\bar{\gamma}^{C}_{\ell,C^{\prime}}=\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}.

(66)

For any other
\ell\in I
and
t\in(I\cup\mathcal{C})
define

\bar{\gamma}^{C}_{\ell,t}=0.

(67)

Finally, define

\bar{\lambda}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{C}.

(68)

We show below that all constraints of the
\bar{y}
-polytope are satisfied for
\bar{\lambda}
. Let
\ell\in I
and
t\in I\cup\mathcal{C}
such that
\ell\notin\textsf{fit}\left(t\right)
. Then,

\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{y}_{C}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{C}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{y}_{C}\cdot 0=0.

(69)

The first equality is by (
68
). Because
\ell\notin\textsf{fit}\left(t\right)
, then
\ell\neq t
by (
2
); moreover, for all
C\in\mathcal{C}
such that
\ell\in C\setminus R(C)
and
C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{w}^{C})
, it holds that
t\neq C^{\prime}
because
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(C^{\prime})
by Lemma
4.2
. Therefore, by (
67
) the second equality follows. By (
69
) we conclude that constraint (
4
) is satisfied for
\bar{\lambda}
.

Let
C\in\mathcal{C}
. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}\cdot s(\ell)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in I~}\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{C^{\prime}}_{\ell,C}\cdot s(\ell)=\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}~}\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{C^{\prime}}_{\ell,C}\cdot s(\ell)

(70)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}~}\sum_{\ell\in C^{\prime}\setminus R(C^{\prime})}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}\cdot s(\ell)=\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}\cdot s\left(C^{\prime}\setminus R(C^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}\cdot\left(1-s(C)\right)=(1-s(C))\cdot\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}=(1-s(C))\bar{y}_{C}.

The first equality is by (
68
). The second equality is by changing the order of summation. The third equality is by (
65
), (
66
) and (
67
). The inequality is because for all
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
, if
C\in\textsf{supp}({\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}})
then
s(C^{\prime}\setminus R(C^{\prime}))\leq 1-s(C)
by Lemma
4.2
. The last equality is by Step
2
of Algorithm
2
. Therefore, by (
70
) we conclude that constraint (
5
) is satisfied for
\bar{\lambda}
.

Let
G\in\mathcal{G}
and
C\in\mathcal{C}
. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G~}\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{C^{\prime}}_{\ell,C}=\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}~}\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{C^{\prime}}_{\ell,C}=\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}~~}\sum_{\ell\in G\cap\left(C^{\prime}\setminus R(C^{\prime})\right)}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}

(71)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\text{ s.t. }C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}})~~}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}\sum_{\ell\in G\cap\left(C^{\prime}\setminus R(C^{\prime})\right)}1\leq\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}\cdot\left(k(G)-|G\cap C|\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left(k(G)-|G\cap C|\right)\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})~}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}=\bar{y}_{C}\cdot\left(k(G)-|G\cap C|\right)

The first equality is by (
68
). The second equality is by changing the order of summation. The third equality is by (
65
), (
66
) and (
67
). The inequality is by Condition 5 of Lemma
4.2
. The last equality is by Step
2
of Algorithm
2
. Therefore, by (
71
) we conclude that constraint (
6
) is satisfied for
\bar{\lambda}
.

Let
j\in I
. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,j}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I~}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{C}_{\ell,j}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}~}\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{C}_{\ell,j}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[j]\text{ s.t. }j\in R(C)}\bar{x}_{C}

(72)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[j]\text{ s.t. }j\in R(C)}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\left(\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}[j]}\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}\right)\leq\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[j]}\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}[j]}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}[j]}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[j]}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}\leq\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}[j]}\sum_{C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}[j]\end{subarray}}\bar{y}_{C^{\prime}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathcal{C}[j]\end{subarray}}\bar{y}_{C}.

The first equality is by (
68
). The second equality is by changing the order of summation. The third equality is by (
65
), (
66
) and (
67
). The first inequality is because for all
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
j\in R(C)
it holds that
\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}[j]}\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}\geq 1
by Lemma
4.2
. The second inequality is because for all
C,C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}
it holds that
\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}\geq 0
. The fourth equality is is by changing the order of summation. The third inequality is because for all
C\in\mathcal{C}[j]\setminus\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})
it holds that
\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{w}^{C}_{C^{\prime}}=0
. The fifth equality is by Step
2
of Algorithm
2
. The sixth equality is by a change in the notation of a variable. Therefore, by (
72
) we conclude that constraint (
7
) is satisfied for
\bar{\lambda}
.

Let
\ell\in I
. Then, we use the following equations.

\sum_{t\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{t\in I~}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{C}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}~}\sum_{t\in I}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{C}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]\text{ s.t. }\ell\in R(C)}\bar{x}_{C}.

(73)

The first equality is by (
68
). The second equality is by changing the order of summation. The third equality is by (
65
) and (
67
). In addition,

\displaystyle\sum_{t\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{t\in\mathcal{C}~}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{C}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}~}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{C}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]\text{ s.t. }\ell\in C\setminus R(C)~}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{w}^{C}_{t}

(74)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]\text{ s.t. }\ell\in C\setminus R(C)~}\bar{x}_{C}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{w}^{C}_{t}\geq\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]\text{ s.t. }\ell\in C\setminus R(C)~}\bar{x}_{C}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]}\bar{w}^{C}_{t}\geq\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]\text{ s.t. }\ell\in C\setminus R(C)~}\bar{x}_{C}.

The first equality is by (
68
). The second equality is by changing the order of summation. The third equality is by (
66
) and (
67
). The first inequality is because
\mathcal{C}[\ell]\subseteq\mathcal{C}
. The second inequality is because
\sum_{t\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]}\bar{w}^{C}_{t}\geq 1
by Lemma
4.2
. Thus,

\sum_{t\in I\cup\mathcal{C}}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}=\sum_{t\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}+\sum_{t\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}\geq\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]\text{ s.t. }\ell\in R(C)}\bar{x}_{C}+\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]\text{ s.t. }\ell\in C\setminus R(C)~}\bar{x}_{C}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]}\bar{x}_{C}\geq 1.

(75)

The first equality is because
I\cap\mathcal{C}=\emptyset
. The first inequality is by (
73
) and (
1
). The second equality is because for all
C\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]
either
\ell\in R(C)
or
\ell\in C\setminus R(C)
. The last inequality is because
\bar{x}
is a solution for (
1
). Therefore, by (
75
), constraint (
8
) is satisfied for
\bar{\lambda}
. In summary, we conclude that
\bar{\lambda}
is a point in the
\bar{y}
-polytope; hence, the
\bar{y}
-polytope is non-empty.

∎

Claim C.11
.

For any
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
, an
{\varepsilon}
-structured BPP instance
\mathcal{I}
, and a prototype
\bar{x}
of
\mathcal{I}
, Algorithm
2
returns a prototype
\bar{y}
such that
||\bar{y}||\leq(1+{\varepsilon})||\bar{x}||
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\|\bar{y}\|={}

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\bar{w}^{C}\right\|\leq\sum_{C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot\|\bar{w}^{C}\|\leq\sum_{C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})}\bar{x}_{C}\cdot(1+2{\varepsilon}^{4})=(1+2{\varepsilon}^{4})\|\bar{x}\|\leq(1+{\varepsilon})\|\bar{x}\|.

The first equality is by Step
2
of Algorithm
2
. The first inequality is by the triangle inequality. The second inequality is because
\|\bar{w}^{C}\|\leq(1+2{\varepsilon}^{4})
by Lemma
4.2
for all
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})
. The last inequality is because
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
.

∎

Claim C.12
.

For any
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
, an
{\varepsilon}
-structured BPP instance
\mathcal{I}
, and a prototype
\bar{x}
of
\mathcal{I}
, Algorithm
2
returns a prototype
\bar{y}
such that for all
C\in\textsf{supp}(y)
it holds that
|C|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10}
.

Proof.

Let
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})
be a configuration. Because
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})
, then by Step
2
of Algorithm
2
, there is
C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})
such that
\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}\neq 0
. Therefore,

|C|\leq|R(C^{\prime})|=|C^{\prime}\cap L\cup U_{C^{\prime}}|\leq|C^{\prime}\cap L|+|U_{C^{\prime}}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-2}+\alpha\leq 2{\varepsilon}^{-6}\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10}.

The first inequality is because the maximal number of items in
C
is bounded by
|R(C^{\prime})|
by the definition of the relaxation vector of a configuration. The first equality is by the definition of
R(C^{\prime})
. The second inequality is by the union bound. The third inequality is since the size of each large item in
L
is at least
{\varepsilon}^{2}
and there can be at most
{\varepsilon}^{-2}
such items in a configuration without exceeding the maximal total size of items in a configuration which is
1
; moreover, by (
9
) it holds that
|U_{C}|\leq\alpha
. The fourth inequality is because
\alpha=\lceil{\varepsilon}^{-5}\rceil
. The last inequality is because
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
.
∎

Claim C.13
.

For any
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
, an
{\varepsilon}
-structured BPP instance
\mathcal{I}
, and a prototype
\bar{x}
of
\mathcal{I}
, Algorithm
2
returns a prototype
\bar{y}
such that for all
C\in\textsf{supp}(y)
it holds that
s(C\setminus L)\leq{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Let
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})
be a configuration. Because
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})
, then by Step
2
of Algorithm
2
, there is
C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})
such that
\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}\neq 0
. Therefore,
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}})
and it follows that
s(C\setminus L)\leq{\varepsilon}
by Lemma
4.2
.
∎

Claim C.14
.

for every
\ell\in I
it holds that
\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[\ell]}{\bar{y}}_{C}\leq 2
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[\ell]}{\bar{y}}_{C}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[\ell]~}\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}=\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})~}\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[\ell]}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}\leq\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})~}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}_{C}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})~}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\|\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}\|\leq\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})~}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot(1+2{\varepsilon}^{4})\leq\sum_{C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{x})~}\bar{x}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot 2=2\cdot\|\bar{x}\|=2.

The first equality is by Step
2
of Algorithm
2
. The second inequality is because
\|\bar{w}^{C^{\prime}}\|\leq(1+2{\varepsilon}^{4})
by Lemma
4.2
. The third inequality is because
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
. The last equality is by (
1
).
∎

The proof of Lemma
3.6
follows by Claim
C.9
, Claim
C.10
, Claim
C.11
, Claim
C.12
, Claim
C.13
, and Claim
C.14
. ∎

Appendix D
Omitted Proofs of Section
5

Proof of Lemma
5.1
:

We prove the two properties of the lemma below.

1.

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus L~}f_{\bar{y}}(\ell)\cdot s(\ell)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus L~}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]}\bar{y}_{C}\cdot s(\ell)=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}~}\sum_{\ell\in C\setminus L}\bar{y}_{C}\cdot s(\ell)=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{y}_{C}\sum_{\ell\in C\setminus L}s(\ell)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{y}_{C}\cdot s(C\setminus L)\leq\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{y}_{C}\cdot{\varepsilon}={\varepsilon}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{y}_{C}={\varepsilon}\|\bar{y}\|.

The first equality is by the definition of frequency. The second equality is by changing the order of summation. The inequality follows since
\bar{y}
holds the conditions of Lemma
3.7
.

2.

Assume towards a contradiction that there is
G\in\mathcal{G}\setminus\mathcal{G}_{\eta}
such that
f_{\bar{y}}(G\setminus L)>{\varepsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|
. Therefore, by the definition of
{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
as the set of groups with maximal frequncies of small items, it holds that

f_{\bar{y}}(G_{\eta}\setminus L)>{\varepsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|.

(76)

We reach a contradiction by the following.

\sum_{\ell\in I}f_{\bar{y}}(\ell)=\sum_{\ell\in I~}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}[\ell]}\bar{y}_{C}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}~}\sum_{\ell\in C}\bar{y}_{C}\leq\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}~}{\varepsilon}^{-10}\cdot\bar{y}_{C}={\varepsilon}^{-10}\|\bar{y}\|.

(77)

The first equality is by the definition of the frequency. The second equality is by changing the order of summation. The inequality follows since
\bar{y}
holds the conditions of Lemma
3.7
; thus, for all
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})
it holds that
|C|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10}
. In addition,

\sum_{\ell\in I}f_{\bar{y}}(\ell)\geq\sum_{i\in[\eta]~}\sum_{\ell\in G_{i}\setminus L}f_{\bar{y}}(\ell)\geq\eta\cdot f_{\bar{y}}(G_{\eta}\setminus L)>\eta\cdot{\varepsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|>{\varepsilon}^{-10}\|\bar{y}\|.

(78)

The first inequality is because for all
\ell\in I
it holds that
f_{\bar{y}}(\ell)\geq 0
. The second inequality is because for all
G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
it holds that
f_{\bar{y}}(G^{\prime}\setminus L)\geq f_{\bar{y}}(G_{\eta}\setminus L)
. The third inequality is by (
76
). The last inequality is since
\eta={\varepsilon}^{-12}
or
{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}={\mathcal{G}}
. By (
77
) and (
78
) we reach a contradiction. ∎

We use the following auxiliary claim.

Claim D.1
.

|{\mathcal{A}}\cup{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}|\leq 2K({\varepsilon})
.

Proof.

|{\mathcal{A}}\cup{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}|\leq|{\mathcal{A}}|+|{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}|\leq K({\varepsilon})+\eta\leq 2K({\varepsilon}).

The second inequality is because
\mathcal{I}
is
{\varepsilon}
-structured, there can be at most
K({\varepsilon})
massive groups by (
10
) and Definition
2.1
. The third inequality is because
K({\varepsilon})={\varepsilon}^{-{\varepsilon}^{-2}}
,
\eta\leq{\varepsilon}^{-12}
, and
{\varepsilon}<0.1
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.3
:

Let
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})
. We show the properties of the lemma below.

1.

\displaystyle s\left(P(C)\right)\leq\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{Q}}s\left(\textsf{last}_{|C\cap\Phi|}(\Phi)\right)\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{Q}}s\left(C\cap\Phi\right)\leq s(C).

(79)

The second inequality is because for all
\Phi\in\mathcal{Q}
it holds that
\textsf{last}_{|C\cap\Phi|}(\Phi)
contains the
|C\cap\Phi|
items in
\Phi
with minimal size. The last inequality is because each item belongs to at most one class in
\mathcal{Q}
.

2.

Let
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(C)
. First, by (
3
) it holds that
s(\ell)\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}
. Second, we use the following inequalities.

s(\ell)\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot(1-s(C))\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot(1-s(P(C))).

(80)

The first inequality is because
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(C)
. The second inequality is by (
79
). Therefore, by (
80
) and that
s(\ell)\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}
we conclude that
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(P(C))
by (
3
).

3.

|P(C)|=\left|\bigcup_{\Phi\in\mathcal{Q}}\textsf{last}_{|C\cap\Phi|}(\Phi)\right|\leq\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{Q}}|\textsf{last}_{|C\cap\Phi|}(\Phi)|=\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{Q}}|C\cap\Phi|\leq|C|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10}.

The first equality is by (
13
). The second equality is by the definition of
last
. The second inequality is because each item in
C
belongs to at most one class in
\mathcal{Q}
. The last inequality is because
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})
; thus, since
\bar{y}
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
3.7
it holds that
|C|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10}
.

4.

First, there can be at most
{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon-10}
classes for each important group
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}
by Observation
5.2
. Thus, the number of classes is bounded by
|{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}|\cdot{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon-10}
. Second, by Claim
D.1
we have a bound of
2K({\varepsilon})
on the number for important groups. By the above,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{Q}|\leq{}

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}|\cdot{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon-10}\leq 2K({\varepsilon})\cdot{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon-10}=2{\varepsilon}^{-{\varepsilon}^{-2}}\cdot{\varepsilon}^{-3{\varepsilon}^{-2}-10}\leq{\varepsilon}^{-6{\varepsilon}^{-2}}

(81)

The first inequality is by Claim
D.1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle|\{P(C^{\prime})~|~C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})\}|\leq{}

\displaystyle\left({\varepsilon}^{-10}|\mathcal{Q}|\right)^{{\varepsilon}^{-10}}\leq\left({\varepsilon}^{-{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\cdot{\varepsilon}^{-6{\varepsilon}^{-2}}\right)^{{\varepsilon}^{-10}}\leq\left({\varepsilon}^{-{\varepsilon}^{-3}}\right)^{{\varepsilon}^{-10}}\leq\left(\exp({\varepsilon}^{-1})^{{\varepsilon}^{-3}}\right)^{{\varepsilon}^{-10}}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\left(\exp({\varepsilon}^{-4})\right)^{{\varepsilon}^{-10}}\leq\exp({\varepsilon}^{-14})\leq\exp({\varepsilon}^{-14})+3K({\varepsilon})-3K({\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 2\cdot\exp({\varepsilon}^{-14})-3K({\varepsilon})\leq Q({\varepsilon})-3K({\varepsilon}).

Recall that for each
C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})
it holds that
|C^{\prime}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10}
; in addition, by (
13
), for each item
\ell\in P(C^{\prime})
there is a single
\Phi\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
\ell\in\textsf{last}_{|C^{\prime}\cap\Phi|}(\Phi)
and it follows that the number of distinct options for choosing
\ell
is bounded by
|C^{\prime}\cap\Phi|\cdot|\mathcal{Q}|\leq|C^{\prime}|\cdot|\mathcal{Q}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10}\cdot|\mathcal{Q}|
. Thus, by choosing at most
{\varepsilon}^{-10}
items as described above, the first inequality follows. The second inequality is by (
81
). The last inequalities follow since
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
,
K({\varepsilon})={\varepsilon}^{-{\varepsilon}^{-2}}
and
Q({\varepsilon})=\exp\left({\varepsilon}^{-17}\right)
.

5.

Let
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
. Now,

\displaystyle|G\cap P(C)|\leq{}

\displaystyle\left|\bigcup_{\Phi\in\mathcal{Q}\text{ s.t. }\Phi\subseteq G}\textsf{last}_{|C\cap\Phi|}(\Phi)\right|=\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{Q}\text{ s.t. }\Phi\subseteq G}\left|\textsf{last}_{|C\cap\Phi|}(\Phi)\right|

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{Q}\text{ s.t. }\Phi\subseteq G}|C\cap\Phi|\leq|G\cap C|

The first inequality is by (
13
). The second equality is because each item belongs to at most one class. The third equality is because for all
\Phi\in\mathcal{Q}
the number of items in
\textsf{first}_{|C\cap\Phi|}(\Phi)
is the same as in
|C\cap\Phi|
by the definition of
first
. The last inequality is because by (
13
) every item in
C\cap G
belongs to at most one class and each class is a subset of some group.

∎

In the following we prove Lemma
3.7
. The proof relies on the following claims and definitions and is given at the end of this section.

Claim D.2
.

Algorithm
3
is runs in time
\textnormal{poly}(\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}},|{\mathcal{I}}|)
.

Proof.

Finding the significant groups can be done in polynomial time by sorting the groups according to the small item frequencies, where the the small item frequency of a group can be computed in polynomial time as
|\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})|
is polynomial. In addition, finding the massive groups, all groups with large items can be computed in linear time by iterating over all groups. Thus, the construction of the important groups takes polynomial time and therefore the constructing the classes in Step
3
can be also achieved in polynomial time by (
12
). Furthermore, since
|\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})|
is polynomial, computing the projections of all
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})
is polynomial. Finally, the linear combinations in Step
3
and Step
3
are of a polynomial number of elements; thus, they can be computed in polynomial time.
∎

For convenience, we repeat the construction of
\bar{\lambda}
as given in Section
5
. Recall that
\bar{\gamma}
in the
\bar{y}
-polytope such that for all
\ell,j\in I
,
\ell\neq j
it holds that
\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}=0
. We define below a point
\bar{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{I\times(I\cup\mathcal{C})}_{\geq 0}
and show that
\bar{\lambda}
is in the
\bar{z}
-polytope. Thus, it follows that the
\bar{z}
-polytope is non-empty. For all
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, let
P^{-1}(C)=\{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}~|~P(C^{\prime})=C\}
. We start with constructing a point
\bar{\psi}\in\mathbb{R}^{I\times(I\cup\mathcal{C})}_{\geq 0}
, which is used in the construction of
\bar{\lambda}
. For all
\ell\in I
and
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
define

\bar{\psi}_{\ell,C}=\sum_{C^{\prime}\in P^{-1}(C)}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C^{\prime}}.

(82)

Moreover, for any
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}
,
i\in[\tau(G)-1]
,
\ell\in\Delta_{i+1}(G)
and
j\in\Delta_{i}(G)
define

\bar{\psi}_{\ell,j}=\frac{f_{\bar{u}}(j)}{f_{\bar{u}}(\Delta_{i}(G))}\cdot\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,\ell}.

(83)

For any other
\ell,j\in I
define
\bar{\psi}_{\ell,j}=0
. We use the following auxiliary claims.

Claim D.3
.

\bar{\psi}
satisfies Constraint (
4
) of the
\bar{u}
-polytope.

Proof.

Let
\ell\in I
and
t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\bar{\psi}_{\ell,t}>0
. We split into two cases as follows.

1.

t\in{\mathcal{C}}
. By (
82
), there exists
C\in P^{-1}(t)
such that
\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}>0
. Therefore,
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(C)
by (
4
) since
\bar{\gamma}
is in the
\bar{y}
-polytope. Consequently, by Lemma
5.3
it follows that
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(t)
.

2.

There are
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}
and
i\in[\tau(G)-1]
such that
t\in\Delta_{i}(G)
. By (
83
) it follows that
\ell\in\Delta_{i+1}(G)
. Hence,
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(t)
by Observation
5.2
.

By the definition of
\bar{\psi}
, for any
\ell\in I
, the above cases cover all possibilities for all
t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\bar{\psi}_{\ell,t}>0
.
∎

Claim D.4
.

\bar{\psi}
satisfies constraint (
5
) of the
\bar{u}
-polytope.

Proof.

Let
C\in\mathcal{C}
.

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,C}\cdot s(\ell)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I~}\sum_{C^{\prime}\in P^{-1}(C)}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C^{\prime}}\cdot s(\ell)=\sum_{C^{\prime}\in P^{-1}(C)~}\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C^{\prime}}\cdot s(\ell)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{C^{\prime}\in P^{-1}(C)~}\left(1-s(C^{\prime})\right)\bar{y}_{C^{\prime}}\leq\sum_{C^{\prime}\in P^{-1}(C)~}\left(1-s(C)\right)\bar{y}_{C^{\prime}}\leq\left(1-s(C)\right)\bar{u}_{C}.

The first inequality is because
\bar{\gamma}
is in the
\bar{y}
-polytope; thus, the inequality follows by (
5
). The second inequality is because for all
C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})
it holds that
s(P(C^{\prime}))\leq s(C^{\prime})
by Lemma
5.3
. The last inequality is by Step
3
of Algorithm
3
.

∎

Claim D.5
.

\bar{\psi}
satisfies constraint (
6
) of the
\bar{u}
-polytope.

Proof.

Let
G\in\mathcal{G}
and
C\in\mathcal{C}
.

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,C}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G~}\sum_{C^{\prime}\in P^{-1}(C)}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C^{\prime}}=\sum_{C^{\prime}\in P^{-1}(C)~}\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C^{\prime}}\leq\sum_{C^{\prime}\in P^{-1}(C)}\bar{y}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot\left(k(G)-|C^{\prime}\cap G|\right)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\left(k(G)-|C\cap G|\right)\sum_{C^{\prime}\in P^{-1}(C)}\bar{y}_{C^{\prime}}\leq\bar{u}_{C}\cdot\left(k(G)-|C\cap G|\right).

The first inequality is because
\bar{\gamma}
is in the
\bar{y}
-polytope; thus, the inequality follows by (
6
). The second inequality is by Property 5 of Lemma
5.3
.

∎

We use the following auxiliary claims.

Claim D.6
.

For all
\Phi\in\mathcal{Q}
it holds that
f_{\bar{y}}(\Phi)\leq\frac{f_{\bar{u}}(\Phi)}{\left(1+\frac{2{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon}}{\|\bar{y}\|}\right)}
.

Proof.

\displaystyle f_{\bar{y}}(\Phi)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\Phi}f_{\bar{y}}(\ell)=\sum_{\ell\in\Phi~}\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[\ell]}\bar{y}_{C}=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}~}\sum_{\ell\in\Phi\cap C}\bar{y}_{C}=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}~}|C\cap\Phi|\cdot\bar{y}_{C}

(84)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}~}\sum_{C^{\prime}\in P^{-1}(C)~}|C^{\prime}\cap\Phi|\cdot\bar{y}_{C^{\prime}}=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}~}|C\cap\Phi|\sum_{C^{\prime}\in P^{-1}(C)~}\bar{y}_{C^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}~}|C\cap\Phi|\cdot\frac{\bar{u}_{C}}{\left(1+\frac{2{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon}}{\|\bar{y}\|}\right)}=\frac{1}{\left(1+\frac{2{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon}}{\|\bar{y}\|}\right)}\cdot\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}~}|C\cap\Phi|\cdot\bar{u}_{C}.

The sixth equality is because for all
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
it holds that
|C\cap\Phi|=|P(C)\cap\Phi|
by (
13
). The last inequality is by Step
3
of Algorithm
3
. Additionally,

\displaystyle f_{\bar{u}}(\Phi)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\Phi}f_{\bar{u}}(\ell)=\sum_{\ell\in\Phi~}\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[\ell]}\bar{u}_{C}=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}~}\sum_{\ell\in\Phi\cap C}\bar{u}_{C}=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}~}|C\cap\Phi|\cdot\bar{u}_{C}

(85)

The claim follows by (
84
) and (
85
).
∎

Claim D.7
.

Let
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}
,
i\in[\tau(G)-1]
and let
j\in\Delta_{i}(G)
. It holds that
\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,j}\leq f_{\bar{u}}(j)
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,j}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\Delta_{i+1}(G)}\frac{f_{\bar{u}}(j)}{f_{\bar{u}}(\Delta_{i}(G))}\cdot\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,\ell}\leq\sum_{\ell\in\Delta_{i+1}(G)}\frac{f_{\bar{u}}(j)}{f_{\bar{u}}(\Delta_{i}(G))}\cdot f_{\bar{y}}(\ell)\cdot=\frac{f_{\bar{u}}(j)}{f_{\bar{u}}(\Delta_{i}(G))}\cdot f_{\bar{y}}(\Delta_{i+1}(G))

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{f_{\bar{u}}(j)}{f_{\bar{y}}(\Delta_{i}(G))\cdot\left(1+\frac{2{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon}}{\|\bar{y}\|}\right)}\cdot{f_{\bar{y}}(\Delta_{i+1}(G))}{}\leq\frac{f_{\bar{u}}(j)}{{\varepsilon}^{\upsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|\cdot\left(1+\frac{2{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon}}{\|\bar{y}\|}\right)}\cdot{\left({\varepsilon}^{\upsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+2\right)}{}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{\bar{u}}(j)=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[j]}\bar{u}_{C}.

The first equality is by (
83
). The second inequality is by Claim
D.6
. The third inequality is by Observation
5.2
.

∎

The following observation follows from Claim
D.7
, that cover the only case where for some
\ell,j\in I
it holds that
\bar{\psi}_{\ell,j}>0
by (
83
).

Observation D.8
.

\bar{\psi}
satisfies constraint (
7
) of the
\bar{u}
-polytope.

Proof of Claim
5.4
:

Claim
5.4
follows from Claim
D.3
, Claim
D.4
, Claim
D.5
, and Observation
D.8
. ∎

We show below that
\psi
satisfies constraint (
8
) for a specific subset of the items, using the following auxiliary claims.

Claim D.9
.

For all
\ell\in I
it holds that
\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,C}=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,C}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}~}\sum_{C^{\prime}\in P^{-1}(C)}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C^{\prime}}=\sum_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}~~}\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}\text{ s.t. }P(C^{\prime})=C}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C^{\prime}}=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}.

∎

Claim D.10
.

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}
and
\ell\in G\setminus\Delta_{1}(G)
it holds that
\sum_{t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,t}\geq 1
.

Proof.

by (
83
), there is
i\in\{2,3,\ldots,\tau(G)\}
such that
\ell\in\Delta_{i}(G)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,t}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\Delta_{i-1}(G)}\frac{f_{\bar{u}}(j)}{f_{\bar{u}}(\Delta_{i-1}(G))}\cdot\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,\ell}+\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,C}=\frac{\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,\ell}}{{f_{\bar{u}}(\Delta_{i-1}(G))}}\sum_{j\in\Delta_{i-1}(G)}f_{\bar{u}}(j)+\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,C}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,\ell}}{{f_{\bar{u}}(\Delta_{i-1}(G))}}\cdot f_{\bar{u}}(\Delta_{i-1}(G))+\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}=\sum_{t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,t}\geq 1.

The first equality is by (
83
). The third equality is by Claim
D.9
. The fourth equality is because for all
\ell\neq j\in I
it holds that
\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}=0
. The last inequality is because
\bar{\gamma}
is in the
\bar{y}
-polytope and thus satisfies property (
8
).

∎

We repeat the definition of
\bar{\mu}
from Section
5
. We define a point
\bar{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{I}_{\geq 0}
as follows. For all
\ell\in I
, define

\bar{\mu}_{\ell}=\max\left\{0,1-\sum_{t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,t}\right\}.

(86)

We prove below some properties of
\bar{\mu}
.

Claim D.11
.

\textnormal{{supp}}(\bar{\mu})\setminus L\subseteq\textnormal{{fit}}(\emptyset)
.

Proof.

Let
\ell\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{\mu})\setminus L
. Thus, it follows that
\ell\in I\setminus L
. Hence,
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(\emptyset)
by (
3
).
∎

Claim D.12
.

For all
\ell\in\textnormal{{supp}}(\bar{\mu})
it holds that
\ell\in\textnormal{{fit}}\left(r\left(\textnormal{{group}}(\ell)\right)\right)
.

Proof.

Let
\ell\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{\mu})
. Thus, by the definition of the maximal slot
r\left(\textnormal{{group}}(\ell)\right)
it holds that
s(\ell)\leq s\left(r\left(\textnormal{{group}}(\ell)\right)\right)
and that
\textnormal{{group}}(\ell)=r\left(\textnormal{{group}}(\ell)\right)
. Hence, the claim follows by (
2
).
∎

We use the following auxiliary claim.

Claim D.13
.

For all
S\subseteq I
it holds that
\sum_{\ell\in S}\bar{\mu}_{\ell}\leq f_{\bar{y}}(S)
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in S}\bar{\mu}_{\ell}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in S}\max\left\{0,1-\sum_{t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,t}\right\}\leq\sum_{\ell\in S}\max\left\{0,1-\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,C}\right\}=\sum_{\ell\in S}\max\left\{0,1-\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in S}\max\left\{0,\sum_{j\in I}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}\right\}=\sum_{\ell\in S}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,\ell}\leq\sum_{\ell\in S}f_{\bar{y}}(\ell)=f_{\bar{y}}(S).

The second equality is by Claim
D.9
. The second inequality is because
\bar{\gamma}
is in the
\bar{y}
-polytope; thus, the inequality follows by (
8
).
∎

Claim D.14
.

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}
it holds that
\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\mu}_{\ell}\leq{\varepsilon}^{\upsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+2
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\mu}_{\ell}=\sum_{\ell\in\Delta_{1}(G)}\bar{\mu}_{\ell}\leq{}

\displaystyle f_{\bar{y}}\left(\Delta_{1}(G)\right)\leq{\varepsilon}^{\upsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+2.

The first equality is by Claim
D.10
and (
86
). The first inequality is by Claim
D.13
. The second inequality is by Observation
5.2
.
∎

Claim D.15
.

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\mu}_{\ell}\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+2
.

Proof.

We split the proof into two cases.

1.

G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}
. The claim follows by Claim
D.14
.

2.

G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus({\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}})
. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\mu}_{\ell}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G\setminus L}\bar{\mu}_{\ell}\leq f_{\bar{y}}\left(G\setminus L\right)\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+2.

The first equality is because
G\notin{\mathcal{A}}
; thus, it follows that
L\cap G=\emptyset
by (
10
). The first inequality is by Claim
D.13
. The second inequality is by Lemma
5.1
.

∎

Claim D.16
.

\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus L}\bar{\mu}_{\ell}\cdot s(\ell)\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|
.

Proof.

We use the following inequalities. First,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus L}\bar{\mu}_{\ell}\cdot s(\ell)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus L}\max\left\{0,1-\sum_{t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,t}\right\}\cdot s(\ell)

(87)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus L}\max\left\{0,1-\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,C}\right\}\cdot s(\ell)=\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus L}\max\left\{0,1-\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}\right\}\cdot s(\ell)

The last equality is by Claim
D.9
. Second,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus L}\max\left\{0,1-\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}\right\}\cdot s(\ell)\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus L}\max\left\{0,\sum_{j\in I}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}\right\}\cdot s(\ell)=\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus L}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,\ell}\cdot s(\ell)

(88)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus L}f_{\bar{y}}(\ell)\cdot s(\ell)\leq{\varepsilon}\|\bar{y}\|.

The first inequality is because
\bar{\gamma}
is in the
\bar{y}
-polytope; thus, the inequality follows by (
8
). The last inequality is by Lemma
5.1
. The claim follows by (
87
) and (
88
).

∎

Proof of Claim
5.5
:

The proof follows by Claim
D.11
, Claim
D.15
, and Claim
D.16
. ∎

We Define another point
\bar{\rho}\in\mathbb{R}^{I\times(I\cup\mathcal{C})}_{\geq 0}
based on
\bar{\mu}
. For all
\ell\in I\setminus L
define
\bar{\rho}_{\ell,\emptyset}=\bar{\mu}_{\ell}
; in addition, for all
G\in{\mathcal{G}},\ell\in G
define
\bar{\rho}_{\ell,r(G)}=\bar{\mu}_{\ell}
; for any other entry
(\ell,t)\in I\times\left(I\cup{\mathcal{C}}\right)
define
\bar{\rho}_{\ell,t}=0
. Finally, using the definitions of
\bar{\rho}
and
\bar{\psi}
we define:

\bar{\lambda}=\bar{\rho}+\bar{\psi}.

(89)

Claim D.17
.

\bar{\lambda}
satisfies constraint (
4
) of the
\bar{z}
-polytope.

Proof.

Let
\ell\in I
and
t\in I\cup{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,t}>0
. We split into three cases.

1.

\ell\in I\setminus L
and
\bar{\rho}_{\ell,t}>0
. By the definition of
\bar{\rho}
it follows that
t=\emptyset
. In addition,
\bar{\rho}_{\ell,\emptyset}=\bar{\mu}_{\ell}
and it follows that
\ell\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{\mu})
. Therefore, by Lemma
D.11
it holds that
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(t)
.

2.

\ell\in L
and
\bar{\rho}_{\ell,t}>0
. By the definition of
\bar{\rho}
it follows that
t=r\left(\textsf{group}(\ell)\right)
. In addition,
\bar{\rho}_{\ell,r\left(\textsf{group}(\ell)\right)}=\bar{\mu}_{\ell}
and it follows that
\ell\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{\mu})
. Therefore, by Lemma
D.12
it holds that
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(t)
.

3.

\bar{\psi}_{\ell,t}>0
. It follows that
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(t)
by Claim
5.4
.

By (
89
), the above proves the claim.
∎

Claim D.18
.

\bar{\lambda}
satisfies constraint (
5
) of the
\bar{z}
-polytope.

Proof.

Let
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
. We split into two cases.

1.

C\neq\emptyset
.

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,C}\cdot s(\ell)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I}\left(\bar{\rho}_{\ell,C}+\bar{\psi}_{\ell,C}\right)\cdot s(\ell)=\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,C}\cdot s(\ell)\leq(1-s(C))\bar{u}_{C}\leq(1-s(C))\bar{z}_{C}.

The second equality is because for all
\ell\in I
and
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\{\emptyset\}
it holds that
\bar{\rho}_{\ell,C^{\prime}}=0
. The first inequality is because
\bar{\psi}
satisfies constraint (
5
) of the
\bar{u}
-polytope by Claim
5.4
. The second inequality is by Step
3
in Algorithm
3
.

2.

C=\emptyset
.

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\lambda}_{\ell,\emptyset}\cdot s(\ell)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I}\left(\bar{\rho}_{\ell,\emptyset}+\bar{\psi}_{\ell,\emptyset}\right)\cdot s(\ell)=\sum_{\ell\in I\setminus L}\bar{\mu}_{\ell}\cdot s(\ell)+\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\psi}_{\ell,\emptyset}\cdot s(\ell)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\varepsilon}\|\bar{y}\|+(1-s(\emptyset))\bar{u}_{\emptyset}={\varepsilon}\|\bar{y}\|+\bar{u}_{\emptyset}\leq\bar{z}_{\emptyset}=(1-s(\emptyset))\cdot\bar{z}_{\emptyset}.

The first inequality is by Claim
D.16
and because
\bar{\psi}
satisfies constraint (
5
) of the
\bar{u}
-polytope by Claim
5.4
. The second inequality is by Step
3
in Algorithm
3
.

∎

Otherwise,

The first inequality is because

) of the

Claim D.21
.

Claim D.22
.

Algorithm
3
returns a prototype
\bar{z}
of
\mathcal{I}
such that
|\textnormal{{supp}}(\bar{z})|\leq Q({\varepsilon})
.

Proof.

\displaystyle|\textsf{supp}(\bar{z})|\leq{}

\displaystyle|\{P(C)~|~C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})\}|+1+|{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}|

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle Q({\varepsilon})-3K({\varepsilon})+|{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}|+1\leq Q({\varepsilon})-3K({\varepsilon})+2K({\varepsilon})+1\leq Q({\varepsilon}).

The first inequality is because for all
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{z})
either
C=\emptyset
, there is
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}
such that
C=r(G)
, or there is
C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})
such that
P(C^{\prime})=C
by Step
3
and Step
3
of Algorithm
3
. The second inequality is by Lemma
5.3
. The third inequality is by Claim
D.1
.
∎

Claim D.23
.

Algorithm
3
returns a prototype
\bar{z}
of
\mathcal{I}
such that
\|\bar{z}\|\leq(1+5{\varepsilon})\|\bar{y}\|+Q({\varepsilon})
.

Proof.

We use the following inequalities.

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}}\left({\varepsilon}^{\upsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+2\right)\cdot\mathbb{I}^{r(G)}\right\|\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}}\left({\varepsilon}^{\upsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+2\right)\cdot\|\mathbb{I}^{r(G)}\|

(90)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}|\cdot({\varepsilon}^{\upsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+2)\leq 2K({\varepsilon})\cdot({\varepsilon}^{\upsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+2)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\varepsilon}^{-2{\varepsilon}^{-2}}\cdot{\varepsilon}^{3{\varepsilon}^{-2}}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+4K({\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\varepsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+4K({\varepsilon}).

The first inequality is by the triangle inequality. The second inequality is by Claim
D.1
.

\displaystyle\left\|\left(4{\varepsilon}\|\bar{y}\|+{\varepsilon}^{-3}\right)\cdot\mathbb{I}^{\emptyset}+\left(1+\frac{2{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon}}{\|\bar{y}\|}\right)\sum_{C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})}\bar{y}_{C}\cdot\mathbb{I}^{P(C)}\right\|

(91)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\left\|\left(4{\varepsilon}\|\bar{y}\|+{\varepsilon}^{-3}\right)\ \cdot\mathbb{I}^{\emptyset}\right\|+\left(1+\frac{2{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon}}{\|\bar{y}\|}\right)\left\|\sum_{C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})}\bar{y}_{C}\cdot\mathbb{I}^{P(C)}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\left(4{\varepsilon}\|\bar{y}\|+{\varepsilon}^{-3}\right)\cdot\|\mathbb{I}^{\emptyset}\|+\left(1+\frac{2{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon}}{\|\bar{y}\|}\right)\sum_{C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})}\bar{y}_{C}\cdot\|\mathbb{I}^{P(C)}\|

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 4{\varepsilon}\|\bar{y}\|+{\varepsilon}^{-3}+\left(1+\frac{2{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon}}{\|\bar{y}\|}\right)\|\bar{y}\|=(1+4{\varepsilon})\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+2{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{-3}

The first inequality is by the triangle inequality. The third equality is since for all
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
it holds that
\|\mathbb{I}^{C}\|=1
by the definition of the indicator of
C
. Now,

\displaystyle\|\bar{z}\|\leq{}

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}}\left({\varepsilon}^{\upsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+2\right)\cdot\mathbb{I}^{r(G)}+\left(4{\varepsilon}\|\bar{y}\|+{\varepsilon}^{-3}\right)\cdot\mathbb{I}^{\emptyset}+\left(1+\frac{2\cdot{\varepsilon}^{-\upsilon}}{\|\bar{y}\|}\right)\sum_{C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})}\bar{y}_{C}\cdot\mathbb{I}^{P(C)}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle{\varepsilon}\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+4K({\varepsilon})+(1+4{\varepsilon})\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+2{\varepsilon}^{-v}+{\varepsilon}^{-3}\leq(1+5{\varepsilon})\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+7{\varepsilon}^{-3{\varepsilon}^{-2}}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle(1+5{\varepsilon})\cdot\|\bar{y}\|+Q({\varepsilon}).

The first equality is by Step
3
and Step
3
of Algorithm
3
. The second inequality is by the triangle inequality and by (
90
) and (
91
). The last inequalities are because
{\varepsilon}<0.1
,
K({\varepsilon})={\varepsilon}^{-{\varepsilon}^{-2}}
and
Q({\varepsilon})=\exp({\varepsilon}^{-17})
.

∎

Claim D.24
.

Algorithm
3
returns a prototype
\bar{z}
of
\mathcal{I}
such that for all
C\in\textnormal{
{supp}}(\bar{z})
it holds that
|C|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10}
.

Proof.

Let
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{z})
be a configuration. If
C=\emptyset
or there is
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup{\mathcal{A}}
such that
C=r(G)
that contains a single item, then the claim is trivial. Otherwise, because
C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{z})
, then by Step
3
of Algorithm
3
, there is
C^{\prime}\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{y})
such that
P(C^{\prime})=C
. Therefore,
|C|=|P(C^{\prime})|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10}
by Lemma
5.3
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.7
:
The proof follows by Claim
D.2
, Claim

, Claim

, Claim

D.24
. ∎

Appendix E
Omitted Proofs of Section

Proof of Lemma
6.2
:

For all
\ell\in U
, let
d(\ell)=|\{v\in V~|~(\ell,v)\in E\}|
be the degree of
\ell
in
G
. For all
\ell\in U
there is exactly one
j\in I
such that
\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}=1
because
\bar{\gamma}
satisfies constraint (
8
) of the
\bar{z}^{*}
-polytope with equality; let
\ell^{*}=j
. Therefore, for every
\ell\in U
it holds:

d(\ell)=|\{(C,\ell^{*},k)\in V~|~(\ell,(C,\ell^{*},k))\in E\}|=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[\ell^{*}]~}\sum_{k\in[\bar{z}^{*}_{C}]}1=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}[\ell^{*}]}\bar{z}^{*}_{C}\geq\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in I}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{*}}\geq\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,\ell^{*}}=1.

(92)

The first inequality is because
\bar{\gamma}
is in the
\bar{z}^{*}
-polytope. Define a fractional matching as a vector
\bar{M}\in[0,1]^{E}
such that for all
(\ell,v)\in E
define
\bar{M}_{(\ell,v)}=\frac{1}{d(\ell)}
; note that
\bar{M}
is well-defined (i.e., for all
\ell\in U
it holds that
d(\ell)>0
) by (
92
). We show below that
\bar{M}
is a fractional matching in
G
of size
|U|
.

1.

\bar{M}
is a feasible fractional matching in
G
. Let
\ell^{\prime}\in U
. It holds that:

\sum_{(\ell^{\prime},v)\in E}\bar{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},v)}=\sum_{(\ell^{\prime},v)\in E}\frac{1}{d(\ell)}=d(\ell)\cdot\frac{1}{d(\ell)}=1.

In addition, let
(C,j,k)\in V
and let
v=(C,j,k)
. It holds that:

\displaystyle\sum_{(\ell,v)\in E}\bar{M}_{(\ell,v)}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{(\ell,v)\in E}\frac{1}{d(\ell)}=\sum_{\ell\in U\text{ s.t. }\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}=1}\frac{1}{d(\ell)}\leq\sum_{\ell\in U\text{ s.t. }\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}=1}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}\cdot\frac{1}{d(\ell)}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in U\text{ s.t. }\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}=1}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}\cdot\frac{1}{\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in I}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell^{\prime},j}}\leq\frac{1}{\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in I}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell^{\prime},j}}\cdot\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,j}=1.

The second inequality is by (
92
). By the above, we conclude that
\bar{M}
is a feasible fractional matching in
G
.

2.

|\bar{M}|=|U|
.

|\bar{M}|=\sum_{e\in E}\bar{M}_{e}=\sum_{\ell\in U}\sum_{(\ell,v)\in E}\bar{M}_{e}=\sum_{\ell\in U}\sum_{(\ell,v)\in E}\frac{1}{d(\ell)}=\sum_{\ell\in U}\frac{d(\ell)}{d(\ell)}=|U|.

By the above,
\bar{M}
is a fractional matching in
G
with size
|U|
. Since
G
is a bipartite graph, there is an integral matching with size
|U|
in
G

[
45
]
. ∎

Proof of Lemma
6.3
:

For all
\ell\in A_{C,k}
there is exactly one
j\in C
such that
(\ell,(C,j,k))\in M
by the definition of
A_{C,k}
and because
M
is a matching; we define
\ell^{*}=j
to be the
slot of

\ell
. We construct an injective function
\sigma:A_{C,k}\rightarrow C
such that for all
\ell\in A_{C,k}
define
\sigma(\ell)=\ell^{*}
. By the above, for all
\ell\in A_{C,k}
it holds that
\ell^{*}
is well defined and it follows that
\sigma
is a function. Let
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in A_{C,k}
such that
\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
. Since
M
is a matching, it follows that
\ell^{*}_{1}\neq\ell^{*}_{2}
; hence,
\sigma(\ell_{1})\neq\sigma(\ell_{2})
and we conclude that
\sigma
is injective. Finally, let
\ell\in A_{C,k}
. Since
(\ell,(C,\ell^{*},k))\in M
, by the definition of
E
it follows that
\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,\ell^{*}}=1
; thus,
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(\ell^{*})
by (
4
) since
\bar{\gamma}
is in the
\bar{y}
-polytope. ∎

Proof of Claim
6.4
:

We prove the three properties of the claim below.

1.

D_{C}\subseteq\textsf{fit}(C)
: Let
\ell\in D_{C}
. By the definition of
D_{C}
it holds that
\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}=1
, and in particular
\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}>0
; thus, since
\bar{\gamma}
is in the
\bar{z}^{*}
-polytope it follows by (
4
) that
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(C)
.

2.

s(D_{C})\leq\left(1-s(C)\right)\cdot|B_{C}|
:

s(D_{C})=\sum_{\ell\in I\text{ s.t. }\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}=1}s(\ell)=\sum_{\ell\in I\text{ s.t. }\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}=1}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}\cdot s(\ell)\leq\sum_{\ell\in I}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}\cdot s(\ell)\leq(1-s(C))\bar{z}^{*}_{C}\leq\left(1-s(C)\right)\cdot|B_{C}|.

The second inequality is because
\bar{\gamma}
is in the
\bar{z}^{*}
-polytope; thus, the inequality follows by (
5
). The last inequality is by (
15
).

3.

|D_{C}\cap G|\leq|B_{C}|\cdot\left(k(G)-|G\cap C|\right)
:

\displaystyle|D_{C}\cap G|={}

\displaystyle\left|\bigcup_{\ell\in I\text{ s.t. }\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}=1}\{\ell\}\cap G\right|\leq\sum_{\ell\in I\text{ s.t. }\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}=1}\left|\{\ell\}\cap G\right|=\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}=1}\left|\{\ell\}\right|

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}=1}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}\leq\sum_{\ell\in G}\bar{\gamma}_{\ell,C}\leq\bar{z}^{*}_{C}\cdot\left(k(G)-|G\cap C|\right)\leq|B_{C}|\cdot\left(k(G)-|G\cap C|\right).

The third inequality is because
\bar{\gamma}
is in the
\bar{z}^{*}
-polytope; thus, the inequality follows by (
6
). The last inequality is by (
15
). ∎

Proof of Lemma
6.5
:

We divide the proof of Lemma
6.5
to the following claims. For simplicity, let
A^{*}=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m})
.

Claim E.1
.

A^{*}
is a packing.

Proof.

Let
i\in[m]
. By (
14
), we split into two cases.

1.

A_{i}\in F
. Then, by the definition of
F
it holds that
A_{i}
is a singleton, a set containing a single item. Thus,
s\left(A_{i}\right)\leq 1
and for all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
|G\cap A^{*}_{b}|\leq 1\leq k(G)
.

2.

There are
C\in C^{*}
and
k\in[\bar{z}^{*}_{C^{*}}]
such that
A_{i}=A_{C,k}
. Therefore, by Lemma
6.3
it holds that
A^{*}_{b}
is allowed in
C
. Hence, there is an injective function
\sigma:A_{i}\rightarrow C
such that for all
\ell\in A_{i}
it holds that
\ell\in\textsf{fit}\left(\sigma(\ell)\right)
. Therefore,

(a)

s(A_{i})\leq 1
.

s(A_{i})=\sum_{\ell\in A_{i}}s(\ell)\leq\sum_{\ell\in A_{i}}s\left(\sigma(\ell)\right)\leq\sum_{\ell\in C}s(\ell)=s(C)\leq 1.

The first inequality is because for all
\ell\in A_{i}
it holds that
\ell\in\textsf{fit}\left(\sigma(\ell)\right)
; thus, the inequality follows by (
2
). The second inequality is because
\sigma
is injective. The last inequality is because
C
is a configuration.

(b)

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
|A_{i}\cap G|\leq k(G)
. Let
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
. Now,

|A_{i}\cap G|=\left|\bigcup_{\ell\in A_{i}}\{\ell\}\cap G\right|=\bigcup_{\ell\in A_{i}}\left|\{\ell\}\cap G\right|\leq\bigcup_{\ell\in A_{i}}\left|\{\sigma(\ell)\}\cap G\right|\leq\left|\bigcup_{\ell\in C}\{\ell\}\cap G\right|=|C\cap G|\leq k(G).

The first inequality is because
\sigma
is injective and that
G\cap M=\emptyset
; thus, by (
2
) for all
\ell\in A_{i}\cap G
it holds that
\sigma(\ell)\in A_{i}\cap G
. The second inequality is because
\sigma
is injective. The last inequality is because
C
is a configuration.

∎

Claim E.2
.

|\mathcal{H}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-22}Q^{2}({\varepsilon})

Proof.

|\mathcal{H}|=\left|\textsf{supp}(\bar{z}^{*})\cup F\right|\leq\left|\textsf{supp}(\bar{z}^{*})\right|+\left|F\right|\leq|\textsf{supp}(\bar{z}^{*})|+{\varepsilon}^{-21}|\textsf{supp}(\bar{z}^{*})|^{2}\leq{\varepsilon}^{-22}|\textsf{supp}(\bar{z})|^{2}\leq{\varepsilon}^{-22}Q^{2}({\varepsilon}).

The second inequality is because
\bar{z}^{*}
holds the conditions of Lemma
3.9
by Observation
6.1
; thus, the inequality follows by Lemma
3.4
. The third inequality is because
0<{\varepsilon}<0.1
and by Observation
6.1
. The last inequality is because
\bar{z}
holds the conditions of Lemma
3.9
, that is,
\bar{z}
is a good prototype.
∎

Claim E.3
.

The size of
\mathcal{B}
is at most

.

The size of

. Therefore, by (

) the size of
\mathcal{B}
is at most:

\displaystyle|F|+\sum_{C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{z}^{*})}\sum_{k\in[\bar{z}^{*}_{C}]}={}

\displaystyle|F|+\sum_{C\in\textsf{supp}(\bar{z}^{*})}\bar{z}^{*}_{C}\leq\|\bar{z}^{*}\|+{\varepsilon}^{-21}|\textsf{supp}(\bar{z}^{*})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\|\bar{z}\|+|\textsf{supp}(\bar{z})|+{\varepsilon}^{-21}|\textsf{supp}(\bar{z})|^{2}\leq\|\bar{z}\|+{\varepsilon}^{-22}|\textsf{supp}(\bar{z})|^{2}\leq\|\bar{z}\|+{\varepsilon}^{-22}Q^{2}({\varepsilon}).

The first inequality is because
\bar{z}^{*}
holds the conditions of Lemma
3.9
by Observation
6.1
; thus, the inequality follows by Lemma
3.4
. The second inequality is by Observation
6.1
. The last inequality is because
\bar{z}
is a good prototype.

∎

\{B_{C}\}_{C\in\mathcal{H}}
is a partition of

. By (

), we split into two cases.

. Then, by the definition of

A_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
; thus, by (

) it follows that

.

∎

.

.

\{D_{C}\}_{C\in\mathcal{H}}

.

, it follows that there is

∎

For any

We use the following auxiliary claim.

Claim F.1
.

For all
C\in\mathcal{H}
and
\ell\in D_{C}
it holds that
s_{C}(\ell)<0.1
.

Proof.

s_{C}(\ell)=\frac{s(\ell)}{1-s(C)}\leq\frac{{\varepsilon}\cdot(1-s(C))}{1-s(C)}={\varepsilon}<0.1.

The first equality is by (
16
). The first inequality is because
\ell\in\textsf{fit}(C)
by Definition
3.8
; hence, the inequality follows by (

).

Proof of Lemma
7.1
:
Let
X=\textsf{tuple}(B_{C}),Y=\textsf{Greedy}({\mathcal{I}}_{C},{\varepsilon})
,
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
,
Y=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{t})
,
S=D_{C}\cup\bigcup_{B\in B_{C}}B
,
Z=X+Y
, and
Z=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{p})
. We prove the following.

1.

Z
is a partition of
S
. Let
\ell\in S
. If
\ell\in D_{C}
, then there is
i\in[r]
such that
\ell\in X_{i}
; therefore, by the definition of
Z
it holds that
\ell\in Z_{i}
. Otherwise, there is
i\in[t]
such that
\ell\in Y_{i}
; therefore, by the definition of
Z
it holds that
\ell\in Z_{i}
.

2.

For all
i\in[p]
it holds that
s(Z_{i})\leq 1
. If
D_{C}=\emptyset
then the claim is satisfied because
s(Z_{i})=s(X_{i})\leq s(C)\leq 1
; The first inequality is because
X_{i}\in B_{C}
and thus
X_{i}
is allowed in
C
; The second inequality is because
C
is a configuration. Otherwise, If
i\leq t
and
i\leq r
:

s(Z_{i})=s(X_{i})+s(Y_{i})\leq s(C)+s(Y_{i})\leq s(C)+(1-s(C))\cdot s_{C}(Y_{i})\leq s(C)+1-s(C)=1.

The first inequality is because by Definition
3.8
it holds that
X_{i}
is allowed in
C
. The second inequality is by (
16
). The third inequality is by Lemma
1.2
; also, the conditions of Lemma
1.2
are indeed satisfied by Claim
F.1
. Using similar arguments, we can show the claim for
i>t
or
i>r
by the definition of
Z
.

3.

For all
i\in[p]
and
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
|G\cap Z_{i}|\leq k(G)
. If
|G\cap D_{C}|=0
then the claim is trivially satisfied because
X_{i}
is allowed in the configuration
C
. Otherwise, if
i\leq t
and
i\leq r
:

\displaystyle|G\cap Z_{i}|\leq{}

\displaystyle|G\cap X_{i}|+|G\cap Y_{i}|\leq|G\cap C|+|G\cap Y_{i}|\leq|G\cap C|+k_{C}(G\cap D_{C})

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle|G\cap C|+\left(k(G)-|G\cap C|\right)=k(C).

The second inequality is because by Definition
3.8
it holds that
X_{i}
is allowed in
C
. The third inequality is by Lemma
1.2
; also, the conditions of Lemma
1.2
are indeed satisfied by Claim
F.1
. The fourth inequality is by (
16
). Using similar arguments, we can show the claim for
i>t
or
i>r
by the definition of
Z
.

4.

It holds that
p\leq(1+2{\varepsilon})\cdot|B_{C}|+2
.

\displaystyle p={}

\displaystyle\max\{r,t\}\leq\max\left\{|B_{C}|,(1+2{\varepsilon})\cdot\max\left\{s_{C}(D_{C}),\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{C}}\frac{|G|}{k_{C}(G)}\right\}+2\right\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\max\left\{|B_{C}|,(1+2{\varepsilon})\cdot\max\left\{\frac{s(D_{C})}{1-s(C)},\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}\text{ s.t. }G\cap D_{C}\neq\emptyset}\frac{|G\cap D_{C}|}{k(G)-|G\cap C|}\right\}+2\right\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\max\left\{|B_{C}|,(1+2{\varepsilon})\cdot\max\left\{\frac{(1-s(C))\cdot|B_{C}|}{1-s(C)},\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}\text{ s.t. }G\cap D_{C}\neq\emptyset}\frac{|B_{C}|\cdot(k(G)-|C\cap G|)}{k(G)-|G\cap C|}\right\}+2\right\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1+2{\varepsilon})\cdot|B_{C}|+2.

The first inequality is by Lemma
1.2
; also, the conditions of Lemma
1.2
are indeed satisfied by Claim
F.1
. The second inequality is by (
16
). The third inequality is by Definition
3.8
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.10
:
For all
C\in\mathcal{H}
let
X^{C}=\textsf{tuple}(B_{C}),Y^{C}=\textsf{Greedy}({\mathcal{I}}_{C},{\varepsilon})
,
X^{C}=(X^{C}_{1},\ldots,X^{C}_{r(C)})
,
Y^{C}=(Y^{C}_{1},\ldots,Y^{C}_{t(C)})
,
S^{C}=D_{C}\cup\bigcup_{B\in B_{C}}B
,
Z^{C}=X^{C}+Y^{C}
, and
P_{C}=(Z^{C}_{1},\ldots,Z^{C}_{p(C)})
. In addition, let
P=(P_{1},\ldots,P_{a})
be the tuple returned by Algorithm
5
. We prove the following.

1.

P
is a partition of
I
: Let
\ell\in I
. Then, by Definition
3.8
there is exactly one
C\in\mathcal{H}
such that
\ell\in S^{C}
; then, by Lemma
7.1
there is exactly one
i\in[p(C)]
such that
\ell\in Z^{C}_{i}
; therefore, by Step
5
of Algorithm
5
there is exactly one
j\in[a]
such that
\ell\in P_{j}
and the claim follows.

2.

For all
i\in[a]
it holds that
s(P_{i})\leq 1
: By Step
5
of Algorithm
5
there is
C\in\mathcal{H}
and
j\in[p(C)]
such that
P_{j}=Z^{C}_{j}
. Therefore,
s(P_{i})=s(Z^{C}_{j})\leq 1
by Lemma
7.1
.

3.

For all
i\in[a]
and
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
|G\cap P_{i}|\leq k(G)
: By Step
5
of Algorithm
5
there is
C\in\mathcal{H}
and
j\in[p(C)]
such that
P_{i}=Z^{C}_{j}
. Therefore,
|P_{i}\cap G|=|Z^{C}_{j}\cap G|\leq k(G)
by Lemma
7.1
.

4.

The size of
P
that is
a
is at most
(1+2{\varepsilon})\cdot m+2\cdot{\varepsilon}^{-22}\cdot Q^{2}({\varepsilon})
:

a=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{H}}p(C)\leq\sum_{C\in\mathcal{H}}\left((1+2{\varepsilon})\cdot|B_{C}|+2\right)\leq(1+2{\varepsilon})\cdot m+2\cdot{\varepsilon}^{-22}\cdot Q^{2}({\varepsilon})

The first equality is by Step
5
of Algorithm
5
. The first inequality is by Lemma
7.1
. The second inequality is because
\{B_{C}\}_{C\in\mathcal{H}}
is a partition of
\{A_{i}~|~i\in[m]\}
and that
|\mathcal{H}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-22}\cdot Q^{2}({\varepsilon})
by Definition
3.8
.

∎

Appendix G
Omitted Proofs of Section
8

In this section we refer to a BPP instance
{\mathcal{J}}
fixed in Section
8
and a given parameter
{\varepsilon}\in(0,0.1]
; in addition, we use
{\mathcal{I}}=\textsf{Reduce}({\varepsilon},{\mathcal{J}})
as in Section
8
.

G.1
Proofs From the Reduction

Lemma G.1
.

For every minimal pivot
w\in\{2,\ldots,{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1\}
of
\mathcal{J}
it holds that
s(I_{w})\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}(\mathcal{J})
.

Proof.

s(I_{w})\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot s\left(\bigcup_{i\in\{2,\ldots,{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1\}}I_{i}\right)\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot s(I)\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}}).

The first inequality is because
I_{2},\ldots,I_{{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1}
are
{\varepsilon}^{-1}
disjoint subsets of items and by (
18
) it holds that
I_{w}
is an interval of minimum total size. The last inequality is because each bin may contain total size at most
1
; thus, the total size of items is a lower bound to the optimum.

∎

Proof of Lemma
8.1
:

If
\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})=0
there are no items and the claim trivially follows. Otherwise, let

{\mathcal{B}}=\big\{G\in{\mathcal{G}}~\big|~|G\cap(H_{w}\cup I_{w})|\geq{\varepsilon}^{2w+4}\big\}

be the subset of groups that contain at least
{\varepsilon}^{2w+4}

w
-heavy and
w
-medium items. The following holds for all
G\in{\mathcal{B}}
:

\displaystyle s(G)\geq{}

\displaystyle s\left(G\cap(H_{w}\cup I_{w})\right)=\sum_{\ell\in G\cap(H_{w}\cup I_{w})}s(\ell)\geq\sum_{\ell\in G\cap(H_{w}\cup I_{w})}{\varepsilon}^{w+1}={\varepsilon}^{w+1}\cdot|G\cap(H_{w}\cup I_{w})|

(93)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\varepsilon}^{w+1}\cdot{\varepsilon}^{2w+4}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})={\varepsilon}^{3w+5}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}}).

The second inequality is because the size of a
w
-medium item is at least
{\varepsilon}^{w+1}
. The third inequality is because
G\in{\mathcal{B}}
. Therefore,

|{\mathcal{B}}|=\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{B}}}1\leq\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{B}}}\frac{s(G)}{{\varepsilon}^{3w+5}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})}\leq\frac{s(I)}{{\varepsilon}^{3w+5}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})}\leq\frac{\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})}{{\varepsilon}^{3w+5}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})}={\varepsilon}^{-3w-5}.

The first inequality is by (
93
). ∎

The next two lemmas combined are essentially the proof of Condition 1 in Lemma
2.2
.

Lemma G.2
.

For
{\mathcal{I}}=\textsf{Reduce}({\varepsilon},{\mathcal{J}})
it holds that
OPT(\mathcal{I})\leq OPT({\cal J})
.

Proof.

Let
A=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m})
be a packing of
{\mathcal{J}}
and let
w\in\{2,\ldots,{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1\}
chosen in Step
6
of Algorithm
6
. We show that
A
is also a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
which concludes the proof. By the definition of packing, we prove the following.

•

It holds that
A
is a partition of
I
. Follows because
A
is a packing of
{\mathcal{J}}
and because that by Step
6
the sets of items in
{\mathcal{I}},{\mathcal{J}}
are identical.

•

For all
i\in[m]
it holds that
s(A_{i})\leq 1
. By Step
6
the set of items and the size functions in
{\mathcal{I}},{\mathcal{J}}
are identical and the proof follows.

•

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{R}
and
i\in[m]
it holds that
|G\cap A_{i}|\leq k_{R}(G)
. We split into three cases by Step
5
.

1.

G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
. Then,
|G\cap A_{i}|\leq k(G)=k_{R}(G)
where the inequality is because
A
is a packing of
{\mathcal{J}}
and the equality is by Step
5
.

2.

G=G^{\prime}\setminus H_{w}\text{ s.t. }G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
. Then,

|G\cap A_{i}|\leq|(G\cup(G^{\prime}\cap H_{w}))\cap A_{i}|=|G^{\prime}\cap A_{i}|\leq k(G^{\prime})=k_{R}(G).

The second inequality is because
A
is a packing of
{\mathcal{J}}
. The last equality is by Step
5
.

3.

G=\Gamma_{w}
. Then,
|G\cap A_{i}|=|\Gamma_{w}\cap A_{i}|\leq|\Gamma_{w}|<|\Gamma_{w}+1|=k_{R}(\Gamma_{w})=k_{R}(G)
where the second equality is by Step
5
.

∎

Lemma G.3
.

{\mathcal{I}}
is
{\varepsilon}
-structured.

Proof.

Let
w\in\{2,\ldots,{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1\}
chosen in Step
6
of Algorithm
6
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\big|\{G{}

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{G}}_{R}~|~\exists\ell\in G\text{ s.t. }s(\ell)\geq{\varepsilon}^{2}\}\big|\leq\big|\{G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{R}~|~G\cap H_{w}\neq\emptyset\}\big|\leq|{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)|+|\{\Gamma_{w}\}|

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\varepsilon}^{-3w-5}+1\leq{\varepsilon}^{-3w-6}\leq{\varepsilon}^{-3{\varepsilon}^{-1}-3-6}\leq{\varepsilon}^{-4{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\leq{\varepsilon}^{-{\varepsilon}^{-2}}=K({\varepsilon}).

The first inequality is because for all
\ell\in I\setminus H_{w}
it holds that
s(\ell)\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}
because
w\geq 2
by (
18
).
∎

G.2
Proofs From the Reconstruction

For the following, let
A=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m})
be a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
and let
F_{A}=(U_{1},\ldots,U_{m^{\prime}})
(see (
22
)). In addition, for all
w\in\{2,\ldots,{\varepsilon}^{-1}+1\}
let
Y_{w}=I\setminus(H_{w}\cup I_{w})
.

Claim G.4
.

For all
i\in[m^{\prime}]
and
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
|G\cap Y_{w}\cap U_{i}|\leq k(G)
.

Proof.

If
i\in[m^{\prime}]\setminus[m]
, then by (
22
) it holds that
|G\cap Y_{w}\cap U_{i}|=0\leq k(G)
. Otherwise, we split into two cases by Step
6
of Algorithm
6
.

1.

G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
. Then,

|G\cap Y_{w}\cap U_{i}|=|G\cap Y_{w}\cap\left((A_{i}\setminus(\Gamma_{w}\cup\Omega_{w}))\cup B_{i}\right)|\leq|G\cap Y_{w}\cap A_{i}|\leq|G\cap A_{i}|\leq k_{R}(G)=k(G).

The first inequality is because
(\Omega_{w}\cup\Gamma_{w})\cap Y_{w}=\emptyset
by (
20
), (
21
), and because
B_{i}\subseteq\Gamma_{w}
by Lemma
8.2
. The third inequality is because
A
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
. The last equality is by Step
5
of Algorithm
6
.

2.

G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
. Then, by (
19
) it holds that
G\setminus H_{w}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{S}(w)
. Therefore,

|G\cap Y_{w}\cap U_{i}|=|G\cap Y_{w}\cap\left((A_{i}\setminus(\Gamma_{w}\cup\Omega_{w}))\cup B_{i}\right)|\leq|G\cap Y_{w}\cap A_{i}|\leq|G\cap A_{i}|\leq k_{R}(G\setminus H_{w})=k(G).

The first inequality is because
B_{i}\subseteq\Gamma_{w}
by Lemma
8.2
. The third inequality is because
A
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
. The last equality is by Step
5
of Algorithm
6
.

∎

Claim G.5
.

For all
i\in[m^{\prime}]
and
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
|F_{A}(i,G)|\leq|G\cap U_{i}\cap H_{w}|
.

Proof.

We split into two cases by Step
6
of Algorithm
6
.

1.

G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
.

\displaystyle|F_{A}(i,G)|=\max\{|G\cap U_{i}|-k(G),0\}\leq{}

\displaystyle\max\{k_{R}(G)-k(G),0\}=\max\{k(G)-k(G),0\}=0.

The first equality is because for any bin
i\in[m^{\prime}]
and group
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
F_{A}(i,G)
is an exclusion-minimal subset of
w
-light items from
U_{i}
such that
|(U_{i}\setminus F_{A}(i,G))\cap G|\leq k(G)
. The first inequality is because
A
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
. The second equality is by Step
5
of Algorithm
6
.

2.

G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
. Then,

\displaystyle|F_{A}(i,G)|={}

\displaystyle\max\{|G\cap U_{i}|-k(G),0\}=\max\{|G\cap U_{i}\cap H_{w}|+|G\cap U_{i}\cap Y_{w}|-k(G),0\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\max\{|G\cap U_{i}\cap H_{w}|+k(G)-k(G),0\}=|G\cap U_{i}\cap H_{w}|.

The first equality is because for any bin
i\in[m^{\prime}]
and group
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
F_{A}(i,G)
is an exclusion-minimal subset of
w
-light items from
U_{i}
such that
|(U_{i}\setminus F_{A}(i,G))\cap G|\leq k(G)
. The second equality is because
\Omega_{w}\cap U_{i}=\emptyset
by (
21
), (
22
), and because
Fill
(A) is a partition of items in
\Gamma_{w}
by Lemma
8.2
. The inequality is by Claim
G.4
.

∎

Claim G.6
.

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
|D(A)\cap G|\leq{\varepsilon}^{2w+4}\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})
.

Proof.

We split into three cases by Step
5
of Algorithm
6
.

1.

G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
. Then, we use the following inequality.

|G\cap U_{i}|=|G\cap\left((A_{i}\setminus(\Gamma_{w}\cup\Omega_{w}))\cup B_{i}\right)|=|G\cap A_{i}|\leq k_{R}(G)=k(G).

(94)

The second equality is because
(\Gamma_{w}\cup\Omega_{w})\cap G=\emptyset
by (
20
) and
B_{i}\subseteq\Gamma_{w}
by Lemma
8.2
. Therefore,

|D(A)\cap G|=\sum_{i\in[m^{\prime}]}|F_{A}(i,G)|=\sum_{i\in[m^{\prime}]}\max\left\{|G\cap U_{i}|-k(G),0\right\}=0.

The second equality is because for any bin
i\in[m^{\prime}]
and group
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
F_{A}(i,G)
is an exclusion-minimal subset of
w
-light items from
U_{i}
such that
|(U_{i}\setminus F_{A}(i,G))\cap G|\leq k(G)
. The last equality is by (
94
).

2.

G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
. Now,

\displaystyle|D(A)\cap G|={}

\displaystyle\left|\bigcup_{i\in[m^{\prime}]}F_{A}(i,G)\right|\leq\sum_{i\in[m^{\prime}]}|G\cap U_{i}\cap H_{w}|=|G\cap H_{w}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{2w+4}\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}}).

The first inequality is by Claim
G.5
. The second equality is by (
22
) and because
A
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
. The last inequality is by Lemma
8.1
.

∎

Claim G.7
.

For all
i\in[m^{\prime}]
and
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
s(F_{A}(i,G))\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot s(U_{i}\cap H_{w}\cap G)
.

Proof.

If
F_{A}(i,G)=\emptyset
then the claim is trivially satisfied. Otherwise, by Claim
G.5
it holds that
U_{i}\cap H_{w}\cap G\neq\emptyset
. Therefore,

\displaystyle s(F_{A}(i,G))={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in F_{A}(i,G)}s(\ell)\leq\sum_{\ell\in F_{A}(i,G)}\max_{\ell\in F_{A}(i,G)}s(\ell)=|F_{A}(i,G)|\cdot\max_{\ell\in F_{A}(i,G)}s(\ell)\leq|U_{i}\cap H_{w}\cap G|\max_{\ell\in F_{A}(i,G)}s(\ell)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle|U_{i}\cap H_{w}\cap G|\cdot{\varepsilon}\cdot\min_{\ell\in U_{i}\cap H_{w}\cap G}s(\ell)\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{\ell\in U_{i}\cap H_{w}\cap G}s(\ell)={\varepsilon}\cdot s(U_{i}\cap H_{w}\cap G).

The second inequality is by Claim
G.5
. The third inequality is because the size of the largest item in
Y_{w}
is at least
{\varepsilon}^{-1}
times smaller compared to the smallest item in
H_{w}
by (
17
).

∎

Lemma G.8
.

s(D(A))\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})
.

Proof.

\displaystyle s(D(A))={}

\displaystyle\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}\sum_{i\in[m^{\prime}]}s(F_{A}(i,G))\leq\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}\sum_{i\in[m^{\prime}]}{\varepsilon}\cdot s(U_{i}\cap H_{w}\cap G)\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}\sum_{i\in[m^{\prime}]}\cdot s(U_{i}\cap G)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\varepsilon}\cdot s(I)\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}}).

The first equality is by (
23
). The first inequality is by Claim
G.7
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.3
:
The proof follows by Claim
G.8
and Claim
G.6
.

Lemma G.9
.

Given a packing
A
for
\mathcal{I}=\textsf{Reduce}({\varepsilon},\mathcal{J})
of size
m
, Algorithm
7
returns in time
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{J}}|,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
a packing
F
for the instance
{\cal J}
of size
\phi
where
\phi<m+13{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})+1
.

Proof.

In the proof of the lemma we use several auxiliary claims. For simplicity, by Step
7
, let
F_{A}\setminus E=(Z_{1}\ldots Z_{m^{\prime}})
,
\textsf{Greedy}(\mathcal{E})=(X_{1}\ldots X_{x})
,
F=\left(F_{A}\setminus E\right)\oplus\textsf{Greedy}(\mathcal{E})
, and
F=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{\phi})
. Also, for the following let
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m},R)=\textsf{Fill}(\mathcal{J},A)
. Finally, recall the definition of
\mathcal{{\mathcal{I}}}
-partition from Section
8
. For
\beta={\varepsilon}^{-w-2}
, we define
P_{1},\ldots,P_{q}
to be the
\mathcal{{\mathcal{I}}}
-partition: a partition of
\Gamma_{w}
by a non decreasing order of item sizes such that for all
1\leq i\leq j<q
and
\ell\in P_{i},y\in P_{j}
it holds that
s(\ell)\geq s(y)
,
|P_{i}|={\left\lceil\frac{|\Gamma_{w}|}{\beta}\right\rceil}
, and
P_{q}
contains the remaining items from
\Gamma_{w}
. It follows that
q\leq\beta
and we define
q({\mathcal{I}})=q
.

Claim G.10
.

F
is a packing of
{\mathcal{J}}
.

Proof.

We prove the necessary conditions of packing as follows.

1.

F
is a partition of
I
. Let
\ell\in I
. By the following three complementary cases, we conclude that
F
is a partition of
I
.

•

\ell\in E
. Then, there is exactly one
j\in[x]
such that
\ell\in X_{j}
by Lemma
1.2
and (
24
); hence, there is exactly one
i\in[\phi]
such that
\ell\in F_{i}
because by Step
7
of Algorithm
7
for all
i\in[m^{\prime}]
it holds that
\ell\notin Z_{i}
.

•

\ell\in\Gamma_{w}
. Then, there is exactly one
i\in[m]
such that
\ell\in A_{i}
because
A
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
. Then, by Lemma
8.2
and by (
22
) there is exactly one
i\in[m^{\prime}]
such that
\ell\in Z_{i}
. Hence, there is exactly one
i\in[\phi]
such that
\ell\in F_{i}
because by (
24
) for all
j\in[x]
it holds that
\ell\notin X_{j}
.

•

\ell\in I\setminus(\Gamma_{w}\cup E)
. Then, there is exactly one
i\in[m]
such that
\ell\in A_{i}
because
A
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
. Then, by (
22
) there is exactly one
i\in[m^{\prime}]
such that
\ell\in Z_{i}
. Hence, there is exactly one
i\in[\phi]
such that
\ell\in F_{i}
because by (
24
) for all
j\in[x]
it holds that
\ell\notin X_{j}
.

2.

Let
i\in[\phi]
. By Step
7
of Algorithm
7
it holds that
F_{i}=Z_{i}
or
F_{i}=X_{j}
for
j=i-m^{\prime}
. We split into two cases based on the latter distinction.

•

F_{i}=Z_{i}
. Then, by (
22
) if
U_{i}=\{\ell\}
for
\ell\in R
it holds that
s(F_{i})=s(Z_{i})=s(\ell)\leq 1
because the sizes of items are bounded by
1
. Otherwise, by (
22
) it holds that
Z_{i}=((A_{i}\setminus(\Gamma_{w}\cup\Omega_{w}))\cup B_{i})\setminus E
. Therefore, we use the following inequalities.

\displaystyle s(B_{i})={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})\}~}\sum_{\ell\in B_{i}\cap P_{j}}s(\ell)\leq\sum_{j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})\}~}\sum_{\ell\in B_{i}\cap P_{j}}\max_{\ell\in P_{j}}s(\ell)

(95)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})\}~}|B_{i}\cap P_{j}|\cdot\max_{\ell\in P_{j}}s(\ell).

The first equality is because
B_{i}\cap P_{1}=\emptyset
by Lemma
8.2
. In addition,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})\}~}{}

\displaystyle|B_{i}\cap P_{j}|\cdot\max_{\ell\in P_{j}}s(\ell)\leq\sum_{j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})\}~}|A_{i}\cap P_{j-1}|\cdot\max_{\ell\in P_{j}}s(\ell)

(96)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})\}~}|A_{i}\cap P_{j-1}|\cdot\min_{\ell\in P_{j-1}}s(\ell)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})\}~}\sum_{\ell\in A_{i}\cap P_{j-1}}s(\ell)\leq s(A_{i}\cap\Gamma_{w}).

The first inequality is by Lemma
8.2
. The third inequality is because for all
j\in[q({\mathcal{I}})-1]
,
\ell\in P_{j},y\in P_{j+1}
it holds that
s(\ell)\geq s(y)
by the definition of the
{\mathcal{I}}
-partition. Then,

\displaystyle s(F_{i})={}

\displaystyle s(U_{i})=s\big(((A_{i}\setminus(\Gamma_{w}\cup\Omega_{w}))\cup B_{i})\setminus E\big)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle s\big((A_{i}\setminus(\Gamma_{w}\cup\Omega_{w})\cup B_{i})\big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle s(A_{i}\setminus(\Gamma_{w}\cup\Omega_{w}))+s(B_{i})

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle s(A_{i}\setminus(\Gamma_{w}\cup\Omega_{w}))+s(A_{i}\cap\Gamma_{w})\leq s(A_{i})\leq 1.

The second inequality is by (
95
) and (
96
). The last inequality is since
A
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
.

•

F_{i}=X_{j}
for
j=i-m^{\prime}
. Then, It holds that
s(F_{i})\leq 1
by Lemma
1.2
.

3.

Let
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
and
i\in[\phi]
. We split into two cases by Step
7
.

•

F_{i}=X_{j}
for
j=i-m^{\prime}
. Then,

|F_{i}\cap G|=|X_{j}\cap G|=|X_{j}\cap E\cap G|\leq k_{E}(G\cap E)=k(G).

The second and the last equalities are by (
24
). The inequality is by Lemma
1.2
.

•

F_{i}=Z_{i}
. Then, we split into two complementary cases. If
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
:

\displaystyle|F_{i}\cap G|=|Z_{i}\cap G|=|((A_{i}\setminus(\Gamma_{w}\cup\Omega_{w}))\cup B_{i})\cap G|=|A_{i}\cap G|\leq k_{R}(G)=k(G).

The second equality is by (
22
). The third equality is because
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
; hence,
G\cap\Gamma_{w}=\emptyset
and
G\cap B_{i}\cap\Omega_{w}=\emptyset
by (
20
) and (
21
), respectively. The inequality is because
A
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
. The last equality is by Step
5
of Algorithm
6
. Otherwise,
G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
. Then,

\displaystyle|F_{i}\cap G|\leq{}

\displaystyle|F_{i}\cap G\cap H_{w}|+|F_{i}\cap G\cap Y_{w}|

(97)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle|F_{i}\cap G\cap H_{w}|+\max\{0,k(G)-|F_{i}\cap G\cap H_{w}|\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\max\{|F_{i}\cap G\cap H_{w}|,k(G)\}\leq\max\{|B_{i}\cap G|,k(G)\}\leq k(G).

The first equality is because
\Omega_{w}\cap G\cap U_{i}=\emptyset
by (
22
) since
G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
. The second inequality is by (
23
): we discard to
F_{A}(i,G)
only
w
-light items until reaching at most
k(G)
items in
U_{i}
. The last equality is by (
22
) and because
G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
. The last inequality is by Lemma
8.2
.

∎

Claim G.11
.

s(E)\leq 2{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})
.

Proof.

\displaystyle s(E)\leq{}

\displaystyle s(I_{w})+s(D(A))\leq 2{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}}).

The first inequality is by (
24
). The second inequality is by Lemma
G.1
and Lemma
8.3
.

∎

Claim G.12
.

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
|G\cap E|\leq 2{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})
.

Proof.

We split into two cases.

1.

G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
. Then,

\displaystyle|G\cap E|={}

\displaystyle|G\cap D(A)|=\left|\bigcup_{i\in[m^{\prime}]}F_{A}(i,G)\right|=\sum_{i\in[m^{\prime}]}\max\{|G\cap U_{i}|-k(G),0\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[m^{\prime}]}\max\{k_{R}(G)-k(G),0\}=\sum_{i\in[m^{\prime}]}\max\{k(G)-k(G),0\}=0.

The first equality is by (
24
). The first inequality is because
A
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
and by (
22
). The fourth equality is by Step
5
of Algorithm
6
.

2.

G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}_{L}(w)
. We use the following inequality.

|G\cap E\cap Y_{w}|=|G\cap D(A)|\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}}).

(98)

The equality is by (
23
) and (
24
). The inequality is by Lemma
8.3
. Therefore,

|G\cap E|=|G\cap E\cap Y_{w}|+|G\cap E\cap(H_{w}\cup I_{w})|\leq 2{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}}).

The inequality is by Lemma
8.1
and by (
98
).

∎

Claim G.13
.

V(\mathcal{E})\leq 2{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})
.

Proof.

V(\mathcal{E})=\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{E}}{\left\lceil\frac{|G|}{|k_{E}(G)|}\right\rceil}\leq\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{E}}|G|=\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}|G\cap E|\leq 2{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}}).

The first inequality is because for all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{E}
it holds that
k_{E}(G)\geq 1
. The second equality is by (
24
). The last inequality is by Claim
G.12
.

∎

Claim G.14
.

\phi<m+13{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})+1

Proof.

\displaystyle\phi={}

\displaystyle m^{\prime}+x\leq m+|R|+x\leq m+{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})+1+(1+2)\cdot\max\{2s(E),s(E)+V(\mathcal{E})\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle m+13{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})+1.

The first equality is by Step
7
of Algorithm
7
. The first inequality is by (
22
). The second inequality is by Lemma
8.2
and by Lemma
1.2
. The last inequality is by Claim
G.11
and Claim
G.13
.
∎

.

The running time of Algorithm

is
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{J}}|,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.2
, Step

can be computed in time
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{J}}|,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
. By (

), the discarded instance in Step

\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{J}}|,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
. By Lemma

, Step

can be computed in time
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{J}}|,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
; also, concatenating two tuples takes linear time in the size of the instance. The overall running time is therefore
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{J}}|,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
.
∎

G.3
Algorithm

In this section we prove Lemma

. Recall the definition of

-partition from Section

. For

by a non decreasing order of item sizes such that for all

contains the remaining items from
\Gamma_{w}
. It follows that

and we define

. In Algorithm
Fill
we start with

empty sets and with

. In each iteration, we try to move an item from

to some
B_{i},i\in[m]
if it is possible to do so without violating one of the conditions of the lemma. When the above cannot be done anymore, we return
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m},R)
. The pseudocode of Algorithm

is given in Algorithm
9
. For the proof, we use the following claims. Let
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m},R)=\textsf{Fill}({\mathcal{J}},A)
.

1

Initialize

,
B_{i}\leftarrow\emptyset~:~\forall i\in[m]
.

2

while

\exists i\in[m],j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})\},\ell\in R\cap P_{j}\textnormal{ s.t. }|B_{i}\cap P_{j}|<|A_{i}\cap P_{j-1}|~\textnormal{and}~|\textnormal{{group}}(\ell)\cap B_{i}|<k(G)

do

3

.

.

7

Return

.

Algorithm 9

\textsf{Fill}(\mathcal{J},A=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}))

Claim G.16
.

The running time of Algorithm

is
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{J}}|,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
.

Proof.

By Step
9
, Step
9
, and Step
9
, each iteration of the while loop of the algorithm takes linear time by trying at most all items. In addition, there are
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{J}}|,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
iterations of the while loop because by Step
9
, Step
9
, and Step
9
in each iteration an item is moved from

to some

and never moved again. Therefore, there are at most
|I|=\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{J}}|,\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
iterations and the claim follows.

Claim G.17

For each

For all

By Step

Claim G.18
.

|R\setminus P_{q({\mathcal{I}})}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})
.

Proof.

Let
j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})\}
, and let
\ell\in R\cap P_{j}
at the end of Algorithm

; in addition, let

. Therefore, by Step

at least one of the following holds for all

.

•
