|B_{i}\cap P_{j}|=|A_{i}\cap P_{j-1}|
.

•

(ii)
|G\cap B_{i}|=k(G)
.

Now, define the set of indices in
[m]
in which the second condition defined above holds.

T=\{i\in[m]~|~|G\cap B_{i}|=k(G)\}.

(99)

We use the following inequality

\displaystyle|P_{j-1}|={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[m]}|P_{j-1}\cap A_{i}|=\sum_{i\in[m]\setminus T}|P_{j-1}\cap A_{i}|+\sum_{i\in T}|P_{j-1}\cap A_{i}|=\sum_{i\in[m]\setminus T}|P_{j}\cap B_{i}|+\sum_{i\in T}|P_{j-1}\cap A_{i}|

(100)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[m]}|P_{j}\cap B_{i}|+{\varepsilon}^{-w}\cdot|T|\leq\sum_{i\in[m]}|P_{j}\cap B_{i}|+{\varepsilon}^{-w}\cdot{\varepsilon}^{2w+4}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[m]}|P_{j}\cap B_{i}|+{\varepsilon}^{w+4}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}}).

The first equality is because
A
is a packing of
P_{j}
in particular. The third equality is by (
99
) and by (i). The first inequality is because
\Gamma_{w}\subseteq H_{w}
; hence, the sizes of all items are at least
{\varepsilon}^{w}
and there can be at most
{\varepsilon}^{-w}
items from
\Gamma_{w}
in
A_{i},\forall i\in[m]
because
A
is a packing. The second inequality is because for each
i\in T
it holds that
G\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
by (
99
); thus, there are at least
|T|

w
-heavy items in
G
because
B_{i}\subseteq\Gamma_{w}
. Therefore, we get by Lemma
8.1
that
|T|\leq{\varepsilon}^{2w+4}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})
since each
w
-small group contains at most
{\varepsilon}^{2w+4}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})

w
-heavy items in particular.

Therefore, using the above for any
j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})\}
:

\displaystyle|R\setminus P_{q({\mathcal{I}})}|={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})\}}|P_{j-1}|-\sum_{j\in\{2,\ldots,q({\mathcal{I}})-1\}}\sum_{i\in[m]}|P_{j}\cap B_{i}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{w+4}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})\cdot q({\mathcal{I}})

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\varepsilon}^{w+4}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})\cdot{\varepsilon}^{-w-2}={\varepsilon}^{2}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})

The first equality is because
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m},R)
is a partition of
\Gamma_{w}
by Claim
G.17
. The first inequality is by (
100
).
∎

Claim G.19
.

|R|\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})+1
.

Proof.

We use the following inequality.

\displaystyle|R\cap P_{q({\mathcal{I}})}|\leq{}

\displaystyle|P_{q({\mathcal{I}})}|\leq{\left\lceil\beta^{-1}\cdot|\Gamma_{w}|\right\rceil}\leq\beta^{-1}\cdot|\Gamma_{w}|+1={\varepsilon}^{w+2}\cdot|\Gamma_{w}|+1

(101)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\varepsilon}^{w+2}\cdot{\varepsilon}^{-w}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})+1={\varepsilon}^{2}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})+1.

The second inequality is by the definition of
{\mathcal{I}}
-partition. The fourth inequality is because
\Gamma_{w}\subseteq H_{w}
by (
20
); hence, in each bin in a packing of
{\mathcal{J}}
there can be at most
{\varepsilon}^{-w}
items from
\Gamma_{w}
since each item is
w
-heavy and has a size at least
{\varepsilon}^{-w}
; it follows that
|\Gamma_{w}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-w}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})
. Now,

|R|=|R\cap P_{q({\mathcal{I}})}|+|R\setminus P_{q({\mathcal{I}})}|\leq|P_{q({\mathcal{I}})}|+|R\setminus P_{q({\mathcal{I}})}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})+1+2{\varepsilon}^{2}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})\leq{\varepsilon}\cdot\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{J}})+1.

The second inequality is by (

) and by Claim

.

Appendix H
Algorithm
Greedy

In this section we give the proof of Lemma
1.2
. Algorithm
Greedy
is indeed a greedy algorithm, which sequentially adds a new bin and tries to (i) maximize the total size packed in the bin while (ii) packing items from groups which have more items w.r.t. the matroid constraint. Some of the proofs from this section are deferred to Section
I
.

For this section, fix a BPP instance
{\mathcal{I}}=(I,{\mathcal{G}},s,k)
and let
\delta\in(0,0.5)
such that for all
\ell\in I
it holds that
s(\ell)\leq\delta
. We use
{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{J}})
to denote the set of configurations of a BPP instance
{\mathcal{J}}
. Also, recall that the operator
\oplus
denotes tuple (or, packing) concatenation. In addition, for any
S\subseteq I
we define
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus S=(I_{S},{\mathcal{G}}_{S},s_{S},k_{S})
as the BPP instance such that

\displaystyle I_{S}=I\setminus S,~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\mathcal{G}_{S}=\{G\cap I_{S}\neq\emptyset~|~G\in{\mathcal{G}}\},

(102)

\displaystyle s_{S}(\ell)=s(\ell),~~\forall\ell\in I_{S},~~~~~~~~~~~k_{S}(G\cap I_{S})=k(G),~~\forall G\in{\mathcal{G}}\text{ s.t. }G\cap I_{S}\neq\emptyset.

Claim H.1
.

For
S\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
and a packing
X
of
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus S
it holds that
T=(S)\oplus X
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
.

Using Claim
H.1
, the algorithm first finds a configuration
S
of
{\mathcal{I}}
and then packs recursively the instance
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A
. Recall that
V(\mathcal{I})=\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}{\left\lceil\frac{|G|}{|k(G)|}\right\rceil}
and let the
promise
of
{\mathcal{I}}
be a maximum bound over
\textnormal{OPT}({\mathcal{I}})
combining a bound over item sizes and over the matroid:

\displaystyle p({\mathcal{I}})=\max\left\{(1+2\delta)\cdot s(I)+2,V({\mathcal{I}})\right\}.

(103)

We show that the proposed algorithm
Greedy
constructs a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
in at most
p({\mathcal{I}})
bins. For doing so, in each bin we must pack enough items from groups
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
which satisfy that
{\left\lceil\frac{|G|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}>p({\mathcal{I}})-1
; otherwise, the packing might require more bins than
p({\mathcal{I}})
. Thus, we define the set of
bounding groups
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})=\left\{G\in{\mathcal{G}}~\bigg|~{\left\lceil\frac{|G|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}>p({\mathcal{I}})-1\right\}

(104)

To guarantee that after packing the first bin by the algorithm we remain with not too many items from some group, we must take certain number of items from each bounding group. Specifically, for each bounding group
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
we define a
bounding subset
of
G
as a subset of items
S\subseteq G
such that
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus S|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
. Also, let
b(G)
be the set of all bounding subsets of
G
. Finally, define a
minimal bounding subset
of
G
as a bounding subset
S^{*}\in b(G)
of
G
such that (i)
|S^{*}|\leq k(G)
and (ii)
s(S^{*})\leq\frac{s(G)}{s(I)}
. For any
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
let
b^{*}(G)
be the set of minimal bounding subsets of
G
.

Lemma H.2
.

For any
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
it holds that
b^{*}(G)\neq\emptyset
.

We generalize the definition of minimal bounding subset for the entire instance. Specifically, define the set of
bounding subsets
of
{\mathcal{I}}
as all unions of minimal bounding subsets of all bounding groups:

b^{*}({\mathcal{I}})=\left\{S\subseteq\bigcup_{G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})}G~\bigg|~\forall G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})~:~S\cap G\in b^{*}(G)\right\}

(105)

Lemma H.3
.

b^{*}({\mathcal{I}})\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

For all
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
let
S_{G}\in b^{*}(G)
; there is such
S_{G}\in b^{*}(G)
by Lemma
H.2
. Now, define
S=\bigcup_{G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})}S_{G}
; it follows by (
105
) that
S\in b^{*}({\mathcal{I}})
.
∎

1

if

I\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})

then

2

3

return
(I)
.

4

end if

5

6

Choose arbitrary
A\in b^{*}({\mathcal{I}})
bounding subset of
{\mathcal{I}}
.

7

while

s(A)\leq 1-\delta~\textnormal{and}~\exists\ell\in I\setminus A\textnormal{ s.t. }|\textnormal{{group}}(\ell)\cap A|<k\left(\textnormal{{group}}(\ell)\right)

do

8

9

A\leftarrow A\cup\{\ell\}
.

10

end while

11

12

while

\exists\ell\in A,y\in I\setminus A\textnormal{ s.t. }s(y)>s(\ell),\textnormal{{group}}(\ell)=\textnormal{{group}}(y),~\textnormal{and}~s(A)\leq 1-\delta

do

13

14

A\leftarrow\left(A\setminus\{\ell\}\right)\cup\{y\}
.

15

end while

16

17

Return
(A)\oplus\textsf{Greedy}\left(\mathcal{I}\setminus A,\delta\right)
.

18

Algorithm 10

\textsf{Greedy}(\mathcal{I}=(I,{\mathcal{G}},s,k),\delta)

Given the definitions of bounding groups and bounding subsets, we describe Algorithm
Greedy
. The algorithm is recursive. The base case is when the set of items
I
forms a configuration of
{\mathcal{I}}
; in this case, we simply return a packing of one bin containing all items. Otherwise, we construct a bounding subset
A
of
{\mathcal{I}}
, which exists by Lemma
H.3
. Then, we try to increase the total size of items in
A
, without exceeding the capacity of the bin, in two ways. First, by adding items to
A
from groups that do not meet the cardinality constraint. Second, by replacing items from the same group between
A
and
I\setminus A
such that the item moved into
A
is of larger size. Finally, we define the resulting bin
A
as the first bin in the packing, and pack recursively the instance
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A
using Algorithm
Greedy
. We give the pseudocode of Algorithm
Greedy
in Algorithm
10
.

The proof of Lemma
1.2
is based on viewing two complementary cases. First, if
A
, the first bin in the packing, has
small
total size of items, namely
s(A)<1-\delta
, then in this case
A
is a
basis
of maximum total size of the matroid over the items. This also holds for the following bins of the packing, or we would be able to increase the size of
A
. Thus, an inductive argument shows that the algorithm finds a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
in at most
V({\mathcal{I}})
bins.

The complementary case is where
s(A)\geq 1-\delta
; then, the total size deducted from
s(I)
by
A
is significant, and there can be at most
(1+2\delta)\cdot s(I)+1
bins satisfying this property. In addition, it also holds that
V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
because we take a bounding subset for
A
with additional items; using the first two cases, it can be inductively deduced that the returned packing in case that
s(A)\geq 1-\delta
is of at most
p({\mathcal{I}})
bins.

Appendix I
Omitted Proofs of Section
H

For this section as in Section
H
, we have a BPP instance
{\mathcal{I}}=(I,{\mathcal{G}},s,k)
and
\delta\in(0,0.5)
such that for all
\ell\in I
it holds that
s(\ell)\leq\delta
. Also, assume that
I\neq\emptyset
otherwise all proofs here are trivial.

Proof of Lemma
H.1
:
We prove the claim by the definition of packing. For convenience, let
T=(S,X_{1},\ldots,X_{m})
and also
T=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{m+1})
.

1.

It holds that
T
is a partition of
I
. Let
\ell\in I
. If
\ell\in S
, then
\ell\in T_{1}
and for all
i\in[m+1]\setminus\{1\}
it holds that
\ell\notin T_{i}
because
X_{i-1}\subseteq I\setminus S
. Otherwise, it holds that
\ell\in I\setminus S
; therefore, because
X
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus S
there is exactly one
i\in[m]
such that
\ell\in X_{i}
and it follows that
\ell\in T_{i+1}
(only).

2.

For all
i\in[m+1]
it holds that
s(T_{i})\leq 1
. If
i=1
then
s(T_{1})=s(S)\leq 1
because
S\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
. Otherwise, it holds that
s(T_{i})=s(X_{i-1})\leq 1
because
X
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus S
.

3.

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
and
i\in[m+1]
it holds that
|T_{i}\cap G|\leq k(G)
. If
i=1
then
|T_{i}\cap G|=|G\cap S|\leq k(G)
because
S\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
. Otherwise, it holds that
|T_{i}\cap G|=|X_{i-1}\cap G|\leq k_{S}(G\setminus S)=k(G)
. The inequality is because
X
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus S
. The last equality is by (
102
).∎

Proof of Lemma
H.2
:
Let
n=|G|
; by (
103
) and (
104
) it holds that
n\geq 1
. Now, let
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}
be the items in
G
in an increasing order of item sizes. Define

\psi=\argmin_{\left\{i\in[n]~\big|~{\left\lceil\frac{n-i}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq V({\mathcal{I}})-1\right\}}i.

(106)

Observe that for
i=n
it holds that
{\left\lceil\frac{n-i}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq V({\mathcal{I}})-1
because
n\geq 1
; thus, it follows that
\psi
is well defined. Now, define
S^{*}=\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{\psi}\}
. We show next that
S^{*}\in b^{*}(G)
.

•

S^{*}\in b(G)
. First,
S^{*}\subseteq G
by definition. Second,

{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus S^{*}|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}={\left\lceil\frac{n-\psi}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq V({\mathcal{I}})-1\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1.

The first inequality is by (
106
). The last inequality is by (
103
).

•

|S^{*}|\leq k(G)
.

\displaystyle|S^{*}|={}

\displaystyle\psi\leq|G|-(V({\mathcal{I}})-1)\cdot k(G)\leq|G|-\left(\frac{|G|}{k(G)}-1\right)\cdot k(G)=k(G).

(107)

The first inequality is by (
106
), since
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
and
{\left\lceil\frac{|G|-\left(|G|-(V({\mathcal{I}})-1)\cdot k(G)\right)}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq(V({\mathcal{I}})-1)
(recall that
V({\mathcal{I}})\in\mathbb{N}
).

•

s(S^{*})\leq\frac{s(G)}{s(I)}
. It holds that:

s(S^{*})=\psi\cdot\frac{s(S^{*})}{\psi}\leq\psi\cdot\frac{s(G)}{n}\leq\frac{k(G)}{|G|}\cdot s(G)\leq\frac{s(G)}{p({\mathcal{I}})-2}\leq\frac{s(G)}{(1+2\delta)\cdot s(I)+2-2}\leq\frac{s(G)}{s(I)}.

The first inequality is because
S^{*}
is a subset of items of
G
where each item in
G\setminus S^{*}
has a larger or equal size compared to the size of any item in
S^{*}
by the sorted order of the items; thus, the size of the average item in
S^{*}
is smaller or equal to the size of the average item in
G
. The second inequality is by (
107
). The third inequality is by (
104
) and that
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
: it holds that
\frac{|G|}{k(G)}+1\geq{\left\lceil\frac{|G|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}>p({\mathcal{I}})-1
. The fourth inequality is by (
103
).∎

Given a BPP instance
{\mathcal{J}}
, let
A^{{\mathcal{J}}}
be the first entry (from the left) of the returned tuple by
\textsf{Greedy}({\mathcal{J}},\delta)
and let
A^{{\mathcal{J}}}_{0},A^{{\mathcal{J}}}_{1}
be the object
A^{{\mathcal{J}}}
after Step
10
and after Step
10
in the computation of
\textsf{Greedy}({\mathcal{J}},\delta)
, respectively. Note that
A^{{\mathcal{J}}}_{0}
is well defined (the algorithm is guaranteed to construct
A^{{\mathcal{J}}}_{0}
) by Lemma
H.3
. For simplicity, we use
A,A_{0},A_{1}
instead of
A^{{\mathcal{J}}},A^{{\mathcal{J}}}_{0},A^{{\mathcal{J}}}_{1}
, respectively, when
{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{I}}
. Now, for the proof of Lemma
1.2
, we use the following auxiliary claims.

Claim I.1
.

For any BPP instance
{\mathcal{I}}=(I,{\mathcal{G}},s,k)
it holds that
A_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
and for each bounding group
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
it holds that
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A_{0}|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
.

Proof.

Observe that
A_{0}\in b^{*}({\mathcal{I}})
by Step
10
. In addition, note that
A_{0}
is well defined by Lemma
H.3
. Then, by (
105
) for each
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
there is
S_{G}\in b^{*}(G)
such that
A_{0}=\bigcup_{G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})}S_{G}
. We show the conditions of the lemma as follows.

•

s(A_{0})\leq 1
.

s(A_{0})=s\left(\bigcup_{G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})}S_{G}\right)\leq\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})}\frac{s(G)}{s(I)}\leq\frac{s(I)}{s(I)}=1.

The first equality is by (
105
). The first inequality is by the definition of a minimal bounding subset of a bounding group.

•

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
|G\cap A_{0}|\leq k(G)
. If
G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
then
|G\cap A_{0}|=0<k(G)
by (
105
). Otherwise,
|G\cap A_{0}|=\left|S_{G}\right|\leq k(G)
where the equality is by (
105
) and the inequality is by the definition of a minimal bounding subset of a bounding group.

•

For each bounding group
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
it holds that
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A_{0}|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
.

{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A_{0}|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}={\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus S_{G}|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1

The first equality is by (
105
). The first inequality is because
S_{G}\in b^{*}(G)
and in particular
S_{G}\in b(G)
; thus, the inequality follows by the definition of a bounding subset of
G
.

∎

Claim I.2
.

For any BPP instance
{\mathcal{I}}=(I,{\mathcal{G}},s,k)
it holds that
A_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
and for each bounding group
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
it holds that
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A_{1}|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
.

Proof.

We prove the claim using loop invariant for the while loop of Step
10
. Let
A(s),A(t)
be the object
A
before and after an iteration of the while loop of Step
10
, respectively. Now, assume that
A(s)\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
and for each bounding group
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
it holds that
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(s)|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
. We show the conditions of the claim below for
A(t)
. By Step
10
, let
\ell\in I
such that
A(t)=A(s)\cup\{\ell\}
. Therefore,

•

s(A(t))\leq 1
.

s(A(t))=s\left(A(s)\right)+s(\ell)\leq(1-\delta)+\delta=1.

The first equality is by Step
10
. The inequality is because
s(A(s))\leq 1-\delta
by Step
10
; in addition, for all items the size is at most
\delta
.

•

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
|G\cap A(t)|\leq k(G)
. If
G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus\{\textsf{group}(\ell)\}
; then,
|G\cap A(t)|=|A(s)\cap G|\leq k(G)
. The equality is by Step
10
and the inequality is by the assumption that
A(s)\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
. Otherwise,
|G\cap A(t)|=\left|A(s)\cap G\right|+|\{\ell\}\cap G|=|A(s)\cap G|+1\leq k(G)
. The first equality is by Step
10
and the inequality is by Step
10
.

•

For each bounding group
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
it holds that
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(t)|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
. If
G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus\{\textsf{group}(\ell)\}
; then,
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(t)|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}={\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(s)|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
. The equality is by Step
10
and the inequality is by the assumption on
A(s)
. Otherwise,

{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(t)|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}={\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(s)|-|\{\ell\}|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(s)|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1.

The first equality is by Step
10
and the last inequality is by the assumption on
A(s)
. Therefore, by Claim
I.1
and the above loop invariant the claim follows.

∎

Claim I.3
.

A\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
and for each bounding group
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
it holds that
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
.

Proof.

We prove the claim using loop invariant for the while loop of Step
10
. Let
A(s),A(t)
be the object
A
before and after an iteration of the while loop of Step
10
, respectively. Now, assume that
A(s)\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
and for each bounding group
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
it holds that
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(s)|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
. We show the conditions of the claim below for
A(t)
. By Step
10
, let
\ell,y\in I
such that
A(t)=\left(A(s)\setminus\{\ell\}\right)\cup\{y\}
. Therefore,

•

s(A(t))\leq 1
.

s(A(t))=s\left(A(s)\right)-s(\ell)+s(y)\leq s\left(A(s)\right)+s(y)\leq(1-\delta)+\delta=1.

The first equality is by Step
10
. The second inequality is because
s(A(s))\leq 1-\delta
by Step
10
; in addition, for all items the size is at most
\delta
.

•

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
|G\cap A(t)|\leq k(G)
. If
G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus\{\textsf{group}(\ell)\}
; then,
|G\cap A(t)|=|A(s)\cap G|\leq k(G)
. The equality is by Step
10
and the inequality is by the assumption that
A(s)\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
. Otherwise,
|G\cap A(t)|=\left|A(s)\cap G\right|-|\{\ell\}\cap G|+|\{y\}\cap G|=|A(s)\cap G|\leq k(G)
. The first equality is by Step
10
and the inequality is by the assumption that
A(s)\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})

•

For each bounding group
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
it holds that
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(t)|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
. If
G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus\{\textsf{group}(\ell)\}
; then,
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(t)|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}={\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(s)|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
. The equality is by Step
10
and the inequality is by the assumption on
A(s)
. Otherwise,

{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(t)|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}={\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(s)|+|\{\ell\}\cap G|-|\{y\}\cap G|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}={\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A(s)|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1

The equalities are by Step
10
and the inequality is by the assumption on
A(s)
. Therefore, by Claim
I.2
and the above loop invariant the claim follows.

∎

Claim I.4
.

V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
.

Proof.

V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)=\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{A}}{\left\lceil\frac{|G|}{k_{A}(G)}\right\rceil}=\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1

The second equality is by (
102
). The last inequality is because for all bounding groups
G\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
it holds that
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
by Claim
I.3
and for all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}\setminus{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{I}})
it holds that
{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
by (
104
).
∎

Lemma I.5
.

If
s(A)<1-\delta
, then Algorithm
10
returns a packing for
{\mathcal{I}}
with at most
p({\mathcal{I}})
bins.

Proof.

We use the following auxiliary claims.

Claim I.6
.

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
|A\cap G|=\min\{k(G),|G|\}
.

Proof.

Assume towards a contradiction that there is
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
|A\cap G|<\min\{k(G),|G|\}
. Therefore, there is
\ell\in G\setminus A
. Observe
A
after Step
10
. Because
s(A)<1-\delta
, it follows by Step
10
and Step
10
that
\ell
can be added to
A
in contradiction.
∎

Claim I.7
.

For all
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
,
\ell\in A\cap G
and
y\in G\setminus A
it holds that
s(y)\leq s(\ell)
.

Proof.

Assume towards a contradiction that there are
\ell\in A,y\in I\setminus A
such that
s(y)>s(\ell)
and
\textnormal{{group}}(\ell)=\textnormal{{group}}(y)
. Observe
A
after Step
10
. Because
s(A)<1-\delta
, it follows by Step
10
and Step
10
that
y
can be added to
A
and
\ell
can be removed from
A
in contradiction.

∎

Claim I.8
.

If
s(A)<1-\delta
then
V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)\leq V({\mathcal{I}})-1
.

Proof.

\displaystyle V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)={}

\displaystyle\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{A}}{\left\lceil\frac{|G|}{k_{A}(G)}\right\rceil}=\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}{\left\lceil\frac{|G\setminus A|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}=\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}{\left\lceil\frac{|G|-\min\{|G|,k(G)\}}{k(G)}\right\rceil}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}{\left\lceil\frac{|G|}{k(G)}-1\right\rceil}=\max_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}{\left\lceil\frac{|G|}{k(G)}\right\rceil}-1=V({\mathcal{I}})-1.

The second equality is by (
102
). The third equality is by Claim
I.6
. The inequality is because if
\min\{|G|,k(G)\}=|G|
then it follows that
V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)=0\leq V({\mathcal{I}})-1
, assuming that
I\neq\emptyset
.
∎

Now, back to the proof of Lemma
I.5
. We prove the lemma by induction on
V({\mathcal{I}})
. For
V({\mathcal{I}})=1
, it follows that
V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)\leq V({\mathcal{I}})-1
by Claim
I.8
and it follows that
V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)=0
. Therefore, by (
102
) it follows that
I\setminus A=\emptyset
and therefore
A=I
. Thus,
I
is a configuration of
{\mathcal{I}}
by Claim
I.3
. Hence, by Step
10
and Step
10
it holds that Algorithm
10
returns
(I)
, which is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
because
I=A
and
A
is a configuration of
{\mathcal{I}}
.

Recall that for any BPP instance
{\mathcal{J}}
we define
A^{{\mathcal{J}}}
as the first entry of the tuple returned by
\textsf{Greedy}({\mathcal{J}},\delta)
. Now, for the assumption of the induction, assume that
V({\mathcal{I}})\geq 2
. Assume that for any BPP instance
{\mathcal{J}}=(J,{\mathcal{G}}_{J},s_{J},k_{J})
such that (i)
s_{J}(\ell)\leq\delta~\forall\ell\in J
, (ii)
V({\mathcal{J}})\leq V({\mathcal{I}})-1
, and (iii)
s\left(A^{{\mathcal{J}}}\right)<1-\delta
, it holds that
\textsf{Greedy}({\mathcal{J}},\delta)
returns a packing of
{\mathcal{J}}
of at most
V({\mathcal{J}})
bins. For the step of the induction, we use the following auxiliary claim.

Claim I.9
.

s\left(A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\right)<1-\delta
.

Proof.

Assume towards a contradiction that
s\left(A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\right)\geq 1-\delta
. Therefore, it holds that
s\left(A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\right)>s(A)
; thus, we reach a contradiction if one of the following conditions hold.

1.

|A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}|>|A|
. Therefore,

|A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}|=\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}|A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\cap G|\leq\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}\min\{k(G),|G|\}=\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}|A\cap G|=|A|.

The inequality is because
A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)
by Claim
I.3
and
{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
by (
102
).
9
9

9

Claim
I.3
is stated and proven for
A
and not for
A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}
for simplicity; however, the exact arguments can be used for proving the claim also for
A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}
.
The second equality is by Claim
I.6
. The above equation is a contradiction that
|A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}|>|A|
.

2.

There are
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
,
\ell\in A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\cap G
and
y\in G\setminus A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}
such that
s(y)>s(\ell)
. This is a contradiction to Claim
I.7
.

Otherwise, if the two cases above are not satisfied:

s\left(A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\right)=\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}~}s\left(G\cap A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\right)\leq\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}~}s\left(G\cap A\right)=s(A).

The inequality is by Claim
I.6
, Claim
I.7
and that conditions 1. and 2. above are not satisfied. By the above equation, we reach a contradiction that
s\left(A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\right)>s(A)
.

∎

By Claim
I.9
we can apply the assumption of the induction on
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A
; thus, Algorithm
10
returns a packing for
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A
with at most
V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)
bins. In addition, by Claim
I.8
it holds that
V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)\leq V({\mathcal{I}})-1
. Hence, by Step
10
, the returned object by the algorithm is
X=(A)\oplus\textsf{Greedy}\left(\mathcal{I}\setminus A,\delta\right)
which contains at most
V({\mathcal{I}})
entries. Moreover, it holds that
X
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
by Claim
H.1
using the following arguments: (i)
A\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
by Claim
I.3
and (ii)
\textsf{Greedy}\left(\mathcal{I}\setminus A,\delta\right)
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A
by the assumption of the induction.
∎

Lemma I.10
.

If
s(A)\geq 1-\delta
, then Algorithm
10
returns a packing for
{\mathcal{I}}
with at most
p({\mathcal{I}})
bins.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction on
p({\mathcal{I}})
. For
p({\mathcal{I}})\leq 1
it holds by (
103
) that
I
is a configuration of
{\mathcal{I}}
: it follows that
V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)=0
by Claim
I.4
and therefore, by (
102
) it follows that
I\setminus A=\emptyset
and
A=I
. Hence, by Step
10
and Step
10
it holds that Algorithm
10
returns
(I)
, which is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
because
I
is a configuration of
{\mathcal{I}}
. Now, for the assumption of the induction, assume that for any BPP instance
{\mathcal{J}}=(J,{\mathcal{G}}_{J},s_{J},k_{J})
such that (i)
s(\ell)\leq\delta~\forall\ell\in J
, (ii)
s\left(A^{{\mathcal{J}}}\right)\geq 1-\delta
, and (iii)
p({\mathcal{J}})\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
, it holds that
\textsf{Greedy}({\mathcal{J}},\delta)
returns a packing of
{\mathcal{J}}
of at most
p({\mathcal{J}})
bins.

For the step of the induction, assume that
p({\mathcal{I}})>1
. We use the following auxiliary claim.

Claim I.11
.

p\left({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A\right)\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
.

Proof.

We use the following inequality.

(1+2\delta)\cdot s(I\setminus A)+2\leq(1+2\delta)\cdot(s(I)-(1-\delta))+2\leq(1+2\delta)\cdot s(I)-1+2\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1.

(108)

The first inequality is because
s(A)\geq 1-\delta
. The second inequality is because
\delta\in(0,0.5)
. The last inequality is by (
103
). Now,

p\left({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A\right)=\max\left\{(1+2\delta)\cdot s(I\setminus A)+2,V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)\right\}\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1.

The first equality is by (
102
) and (
103
). The first inequality is by (
108
) and Claim
I.4

∎

To conclude the lemma, observe the following two complementary cases.

1.

s\left(A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\right)\geq 1-\delta
. Then, by Claim
I.11
and that
s\left(A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\right)\geq 1-\delta
, we can apply the assumption of the induction on
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A
; thus, Algorithm
10
applied on
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A
returns a packing for
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A
with at most
p\left({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A\right)
bins. In addition, by Claim
I.11
it holds that
p\left({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A\right)\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
. Hence, by Step
10
, the returned object by the algorithm on
{\mathcal{I}}
is
X=(A)\oplus\textsf{Greedy}\left(\mathcal{I}\setminus A,\delta\right)
which contains at most
1+p({\mathcal{I}})-1=p({\mathcal{I}})
entries. Moreover, it holds that
X
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
by Claim
H.1
using the following arguments: (i)
A\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
by Claim
I.3
and (ii)
\textsf{Greedy}\left(\mathcal{I}\setminus A,\delta\right)
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A
by the assumption of the induction.

2.

s\left(A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\right)<1-\delta
. Then, by Lemma
I.5
and that
s\left(A^{{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A}\right)<1-\delta
, Algorithm
10
applied on
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A
returns a packing for
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A
with at most
V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)
bins. In addition, by Claim
I.4
it holds that
V({\mathcal{I}}\setminus A)\leq p({\mathcal{I}})-1
. Hence, by Step
10
, the returned object by the algorithm is
X=(A)\oplus\textsf{Greedy}\left(\mathcal{I}\setminus A,\delta\right)
which contains at most
1+p({\mathcal{I}})-1\leq p({\mathcal{I}})
entries by (
103
). Moreover, it holds that
X
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
by Claim
H.1
using the following arguments: (i)
A\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{I}})
by Claim
I.3
and (ii)
\textsf{Greedy}\left(\mathcal{I}\setminus A,\delta\right)
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A
by Lemma
I.5
.

∎

Lemma I.12
.

The running time of Algorithm
10
is
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{I}}|)
.

Proof.

Step
10
and Step
10
can be trivially computed in linear time in
|{\mathcal{I}}|
. Step
10
can be computed in polynomial time in
|{\mathcal{I}}|
by (i) finding the bounding groups by (
104
), and (ii) compute a bounding subset of
{\mathcal{I}}
as follows. For each bounding group
G
we compute a bounding subset of
G
by sorting the items and choosing a minimal number of items according to the sorted order that form a bounding subset of
G
(for more details see the proof of Lemma
H.2
).

the while loop of Step
10
runs at most
|I|
times because in each iteration an item is added to the constructed bin and is not added again by Step
10
and Step
10
. Finally, the while loop of Step
10
runs at most
{|I|\choose 2}=\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{I}}|)
times because in each iteration two items are replaced in Step
10
, one is add to the constructed bin and the other one is removed; for any two items, this can happen at most once by Step
10
. We conclude that the running time of Algorithm
10
is
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{I}}|)
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
1.2
:
If
s(A)<1-\delta
it holds that
X=(A)\oplus\textsf{Greedy}\left(\mathcal{I}\setminus A,\delta\right)
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
of at most
V({\mathcal{I}})
bins by Lemma
I.5
; therefore, the number of bins is at most
p({\mathcal{I}})
by (
103
). Otherwise, it holds that
s(A)\geq 1-\delta
; thus, it holds that
X
is a packing of
{\mathcal{I}}
of at most
p({\mathcal{I}})
bins by Lemma
I.10
. Therefore, the number of bins in the packing is at most
p({\mathcal{I}})\leq(1+2\delta)\cdot\max\left\{s(I),~V(\mathcal{I})\right\}+2
by (
103
). Finally, the running time is
\textnormal{poly}(|{\mathcal{I}}|)
by Lemma
I.12
.∎
